### 4.3 Results

#### 4.3.1 scACT improves both local and global homogeneity when aligning different modalities

In order to evaluate scACT extensively, we adopted various metrics on its first module, multi-modal alignment on diverse datasets. As an example, we extracted a cohort of five human prefrontal cortex brain samples with 27,414 cells with both scRNA-seq and scATACseq and conducted model training and evaluation with the cell-level correspondence removed. Then, the trained model was evaluated from both global and local homogeneity perspectives against other baseline models, Harmony and LIGER. Through this process, a wide spectrum of metrics, such as UMAP, silhouette score, cosine distance, and modality mixing score were used. In general, we found that scACT outperformed existing methods significantly and yielded biologically-interpretable results.

First, for global homogeneity assessment, UMAP and silhouette scores were employed as metrics. scACT showed superior global homogeneity by generating joint UMAP representations with more meaningful and biologically-interpretable clusters (Fig. 4.2A). Neuronal and non-neuronal marker gene expressions overlayed on scACT's joint embedding showed significant enrichment agreed with their cell type annotation (mean T-test p-values &lt; 0 . 001) and were consistent with previous studies [149]. Also, any marker gene only illuminated one cluster, meaning that the UMAP representation was created based on cell types, not other confounding factors, such as input modalities, batch effects, or sequencing depths.

The UMAP colored by modality not only confirmed scACT's global homogeneity, but also proved its great local homogeneity (Fig. 4.2B). Harmony's embedding exhibited significant separation between modalities, lacking consistency. For instance, in the UMAP, scRNAseq clusters, representing oligodendrocyte cells, were distant from corresponding clusters in scATAC-seq, which included endothelial and microglia cells (Suppl. Fig. C.1). This discrepancy likely stems from neglecting batch effects, emphasizing experimental variations over intercellular differences. LIGER demonstrated improved cell type consistency but displayed teardrop-shaped clusters in the UMAP, suggesting susceptibility to technical factors like sequence depth. In contrast, scACT achieved better results, maintaining consistent cell type mapping and forming round or oval clusters, indicating minimal impact from confounding factors. The silhouette score further validated this, with scACT achieving a higher score (0.612) than Harmony (-0.011) and LIGER (0.510). Additional metrics (ARI and AMI) also showed clear advantages of scACT (Suppl. Table 2).

To further evaluate local homogeneity, we tested the closeness of cells within the same cell type. scACT outperformed its counterparts with a significantly lower distribution of pairwise cosine distance across all cell types (mean F-test p-value=0.0), especially in the astrocytes cells (0.026 vs. 0.426 and 0.576). Moreover, a high modality mixing score (F-test p-value &lt; 0 . 05) was observed for all cell types except endothelial and astrocyte cells. In particular, in the excitatory cells, scACT was able to mix the two modalities much better than the baseline methods (0.973 vs. 0.526 and 0.081). We suspect that the less optimal performance could partially attributed to the imbalance of training data and lack of samples, but with more abundant data, we believed that scACT could deliver its local homogeneity to all cell types.

#### 4.3.2 scACT accurately translates the ATAC modality into the RNA modality

Moving one-step further, we evaluated scACT's module 2: scRNA-seq translation performance by examining marker gene enrichments and expression similarity across cell types and observed largely preserved cell type-specific signatures. First, we selected a curated list of marker genes according to previous study [149] and compared the mean expressions between cell types and between the observed and translated cohort (Fig. 4.3A). We found that scACT managed to preserve similar expression patterns with the observed ground truth (mean R 2 =0.914), but also provided strong enrichment (mean normalized value: enriched=0.894 vs. non-enriched=0.122, mean F-test p-value &lt; 0 . 001, see Suppl. Table 3 for details) with the cell type enriched consistent with previous findings [21],

Taking a closer look, we focused on four key marker genesSATB2 for excitatory cells, GAD2 for inhibitory cells, FLT1 for endothelial cells, and MOG for oligodendrocyte cells as they were the well-known marker genes commonly recognized. The UMAP of them agreed with our previous findings, with the translated expression highlighting the mentioned cell types, plus a high correlation between the observed and the translated ( R 2 = 0 . 633, 0 . 603, 0 . 460, 0 . 684, Fig. 4.3B). We noticed that the correlation of FLT1 fell behind other marker genes, and suspected that the reason being the lack of training samples in that specific cell type and possible remaining multiplets (upper-left of the endothelial cluster), but overall, it yielded decent performance.

The distribution across cell types of observed versus translated profiles further verified the accuracy and correctness of scACT (Fig. 4.3C). A significant enrichment of these marker genes across their respective cell types was shown across all selected marker genes (mean F-test p-value &lt; 0 . 0001), confirming scACT's ability to accurately capture cell type-specific features. Plus, the distributions themselves from scACT also strictly followed the observed truth, indicating a robust learning capability. From both the distributional statistics to the actual data of each individual cell, we demonstrated its accurate translation capability from scATAC-seq to scRNA-seq.

(C) Cell type-specific gene expression distribution, Tn5 insertion intensity, peak annotation, and the peak-to-gene linkages from both the ground truth and the scACT-translated scATAC-seq data.

#### 4.3.3 scACT imputes meaningful and interpretible scATAC-seq signals

Figure 4.6: Evaluation of scACT on human PBMC multiome data. (A) Evaluation on multi-modal alignment with UMAP representations of LIGER, Harmony, and scACT (top) and comparison of pairwise cosine distances across cell types (bottom). (B) scRNA-seq translation performance with obserbed and predicted marker gene expressions (left) and the per-cell type distribution (right). (C) scATAC-seq translation benchmark with ROC curves captioned with AUCROC curves (left) and correlations between the observed and translated chromatin accessibility scores (right).

<!-- image -->

Besides the robust scRNA-seq translation capability, we also assessed scACT's scATAC-seq translation performance from the RNA modality. From a computational point of view, we can treat the translation task as a binary classification problem where for each cell, we predict the probability of each peak being accessible. In this way, we adopted the widely-used ROC curves and AUCROC scores as a key evaluation metric (Fig. 4.4A). Overall, scACT exhibited high accuracy, reflected in the area under the ROC curve (AUCROC=0.796). Although we found that our AUCROC was lower than other reported methods [274], we believed the reason being the different tissue of study (lymphoblastoid cells vs. brain cells) and the quality of samples used (curated cell lines vs. post-mortem tissues). We also believed that the cell-to-cell correspondence required in the other model helped largely. However, in the real world, co-assay data were at a scarce and the quality of sample varied dramatically, and thus scACT could be a better candidate providing meaningful translation. Moreover, subsetting the problem into cell type-specific clusters, we observed consistent and reasonable performance (AUCROC=0.780-0.850).

Additionally, viewing the translation problem as a regression problem in the cell type level, we extracted chromatin accessibility scores from both observed and translated data, allowing us to evaluate the correlation between them. scACT demonstrated excellent correlation ( R 2 =0.593) across all data and maintained consistent performance within each cell type ( R 2 =0.501-0.548). These results jointly highlighted scACT's proficiency in translating scATAC-seq profiles, showcasing both accurate classification and preservation of chromatin accessibility patterns.

#### 4.3.4 scACT enables in-silico perturbations to identify enhancergene linkages

To evaluate scACT's in-silico perturbation capability, we focused on SATB2 , a marker gene for excitatory cells (Fig. 4.5A). Feature importance scores were computed using scACT's module 3, and the mean chromatin accessibility of excitatory and non-excitatory cells plotted alongside their respective feature importance scores for the top 5 peaks (Fig. 4.5B). Notably, T-tests demonstrated a statistically significant enrichment on the top peaks between these two cell groups (p-value &lt; 0 . 001) and especially in the most important peak (activity=0.186 vs. 0.013, p-value=0.0), indicating the effectiveness of scACT in simulating perturbations that lead to discernible chromatin accessibility changes associated with the excitatory cell marker SATB2 .

In addition to the test statistics across cell types, we validated the results from scACT and explored peak-to-gene linkages in both the observed and translated scATAC-seq data, focusing on the most contributing peak identified by scACTin the single-cell level (Fig. 4.5C). This approach allowed us to elucidate regulatory connections associated with the perturbation of SATB2 . We found enrichment of signal for excitatory neurons on the exons and especially around the transcription start site (TSS), consistent with previous studies [117]. To our expectation, a strong enrichment was also observed for excitatory neurons on the peak identified by scACT. Further validation using the identification of peak-to-gene linkages in both observed and translated chromatin accessibility profiles (span of 100k bp; pearson test p-value &lt; 0 . 05) supported scACT's capability to not only simulate perturbations accurately, but also reveal potential regulatory mechanisms, providing valuable insights into the functional consequences with little need to conduct biological experiments.

#### 4.3.5 scACT provides accurate alignment and translation in human PBMC

Since scACT is designed to handle various organisms and tissues, we expanded our study to evaluate its performance in the human PBMCs, which has a broader, more continuous spectrum of cell types compared to the brain prefrontal cortex data. This complexity poses additional challenges for computational models. Through extensive benchmarking, we observed that scACT maintained its robust multi-modal alignment and translation capabilities, outperforming baseline models.

Firstly, we scrutinized scACT's module 1, focusing on multiome alignment (Fig. 4.6A) and demonstrated its substantial ability to create joint embeddings. Qualitatively, scACT demonstrated global homogeneity by generating UMAP representations with more meaningful clusters. Compared to Harmony, both LIGER and scACT generated embeddings invariant to input modalities by integrating the two modalities. Moreover, scACT surpassed the baselines in cell grouping when benchmarked against annotated cell types (silhouette score: scACT=0.128, Harmony=0.125, LIGER=-0.076). Additionally, scACT's embedding exhibited an oval-shaped structure, indicating resistance to sequencing depths and batch effects, unlike Harmony and LIGER which generated clusters with sharp edges. Quantitatively, scACT showed significantly closer clusters by cell types, especially in smaller cell types like dnT and cDC1 (mean F-test p-value &lt; 0 . 001).

Next, a comprehensive inspection of scACT's scRNA-seq translation module revealed its improved accuracy (Fig. 4.6B). Analysis of four marker genes ( CCL4 for NK cells, IL32 for CD8 cells, CD14 for CD14 cells, and IGKC for B cells) indicated high specificity with respect to the represented cell types (T-test p-value &lt; 0 . 001), consistent with previous studies [174, 104, 225]. There was a high correlation between observed and translated matrices in most cases ( R 2 = 0 . 433 , 0 . 523 , 0 . 245 , 0 . 095). For the IGKC gene, a potential cause for weaker performance was hypothesized to be the decreased number of cells in the B cell group and further subtypes differentiation, but in general scACT gave great performance. Zooming into cell type levels of the four marker genes revealed a clear preservation of cell type-specific signatures in expression distributions between observed and translated. Compared to outgroups, cell types corresponding to marker genes showed a significant increase in translated profiles (T-test p-value &lt; 0 . 05). Overall, scACT successfully generalized its scRNA-seq translation capability to PBMC tissue.

We also benchmarked scACT's scATAC-seq translation performance on the PBMC dataset (Fig. 4.6C). It effectively reconstructed chromatin accessibility from gene expressions, formulated as binary classification problems evaluated through ROC curves. Overall, scACT demonstrated high accuracy (AUCROC=0.901). When subset to larger or smaller cell typespecific clusters, it performed reasonably well (AUCROC=0.856-0.925). From a biological perspective, we sought the correlation between observed and translated chromatin accessibility per cell and per peak. scACT exhibited excellent performance with an overall R 2 value of

0.828 across all data and consistent performance in each cell type ( R 2 =0.759-0.850). In multiple evaluations, scACT showcased considerable translation performance from transcription profile to chromatin accessibility at the single-cell resolution.

### 4.4 Discussion

Understanding multi-modal cellular heterogeneity is crucial in genomics, and single-cell sequencing data provide great opportunities. However, deeper understanding of the hidden biology requires concurrent analysis of multiple modalities while paired co-assay data are scarce. Plus, the analysis of unpaired single-cell multi-modal data presents significant challenges. In this context, we introduced scACT, a comprehensive model designed to address these challenges by excelling in multi-modal alignment, cross-modality translation, and insilico perturbation. Our model is particularly adept at handling unpaired single-cell multimodal datasets, offering a powerful solution to unravel complex biological interactions.

Extensive benchmarking on diverse datasets, with a focus on brain prefrontal cortex data, demonstrated scACT's superior multiome alignment performance compared to existing methods such as Harmony and LIGER, in preserving both global and local homogeneity in the generated joint embeddings. Plus, we showcased scACT's proficiency in cross-modality translation in two directions: from scATAC-seq into scRNA-seq with multiple gene-level analyses and from scRNA-seq to scATAC-seq using discriminative and regressive metrics. Focusing on SATB2 as a case study, scACT accurately conducted in-silico perturbations, as evidenced by statistically significant enrichment in chromatin accessibility changes associated with excitatory cells, aiding in understanding regulatory mechanisms as well as reducing the reliance on labor-intensive and time-consuming biological experiments Moreover, scACT demonstrated its applicability not only in brain cells, but also in other diverse tissues, such as human PBMCs, maintaining accuracy and consistency in all its modules In summary, scACT offers a comprehensive solution for analyzing unpaired single-cell multimodal data, providing advancements in multi-modal alignment, cross-modality translation, and in-silico perturbation using a weakly-supervised approach with cycle-consistency. The model's robust performance across diverse datasets and tissues highlights its potential impact on advancing our understanding of complex biological systems. Currently, much more singlemodality data are available with relatively low cost and high quality, whereas the emerging joint sequencing technology needs time and effort to mature. We believe that scACT can help the community in robust and accurate mapping, translation, perturbation, and further downstream analysis more effectively using more cohorts of data with higher quality to unlock more intriguing biological mechanisms and regulatory insights of different tissues and organisms.

### Chapter 5

## GRIDS: Learnable Subset Perturbations for Global Regulatory Redundancy Reasoning

### 5.1 Introduction

Cis-regulatory elements (CREs) play a central role in transcriptional control: they help preserve cell identity, orchestrate gene programs, and mediate responses to intra- and extracellular signals. Disruption of these regulatory processes has been implicated in a wide range of diseases [111]. Despite sustained efforts to catalog CREs, it remains difficult to directly validate the functional consequence of a single CRE on intermediate molecular phenotypes (e.g., gene expression) or downstream clinical outcomes [113, 18]. A growing body of work instead supports a distributed view of regulation, where multiple CREs can converge on a shared target gene and produce overlapping spatiotemporal expression patterns; consequently, loss or perturbation of one element can be buffered by others [134]. These coordinated multi-CRE effects are often summarized as regulatory redundancy and are thought to be prevalent across genomes as a mechanism for phenotypic robustness [145]. However, most computational studies continue to prioritize identifying individual CRE-to-gene associations, while systematic analysis of redundancy, which requires reasoning over interactions among many CREs, remains comparatively underexplored. In this work, we develop an in-silico approach to study combinatorial regulatory redundancy at single-cell resolution by linking the problem to global feature-importance explanations for black-box predictive models.

A key challenge is that many existing strategies for dissecting redundancy are tailored to tissue-level assays and are therefore designed around estimating effects of individual CREs, rather than coordinated effects among multiple CREs within heterogeneous cell populations. Early studies demonstrated that apparently redundant elements, such as shadow enhancers, can precisely modulate developmental expression and improve robustness under physiological or genetic stress [86]. Yet these investigations were often single-locus and reporter-based, which limits their ability to provide genome-wide insights [201]. At larger scale, resources and consortia efforts, including ENCODE, have enabled the identification of millions of candidate CREs across tissues [171], and large transgenic reporter assays have characterized subsets of these candidates in vivo [176]. Nonetheless, tissue-averaged measurements can obscure regulatory heterogeneity across cell types and cell states. Recent advances in singlecell sequencing, multi-modal genomic profiling in particular, now provide the resolution needed to study regulation at the cellular level. Computational methods for single-cell data have successfully uncovered complex, cell-type-specific regulatory mechanisms [103, 293]. However, most of these approaches still emphasize one-to-one CRE-to-gene relationships, and typically do not model synergistic, compensatory, or redundant effects that arise from sets of CREs.

To address these limitations, we begin by considering a predictive setting in which gene expression is modeled as a function of CRE activity. Under this viewpoint, identifying multi-CRE-to-gene relationships becomes closely related to feature-importance explanation for black-box models [234]. Existing explanation methods generally fall into two classes: local and global . Local explanation techniques, such as LIME [212] and SHAP [169], focus on explaining individual predictions via instance-wise feature importance. Recent work has also explored differentiable surrogate models that generate explanations by learning minimal adversarial perturbations [46], and [50] proposed jointly training a surrogate model together with its explanations. In contrast, regulatory redundancy is inherently a populationlevel and combinatorial problem: the goal is to identify feature subsets whose effects generalize across many cells, making global feature-importance formulations a more natural match [75, 120]. Yet many global approaches reduce the influence of combinatorial feature sets to additive importance scores, which can overlook nonlinear interactions among CREs. Perturbation-based strategies partially mitigate this by masking features (e.g., setting values to zero) and quantifying performance degradation [228, 169]. Covert et al. [63] further proposed a sampling-based approximation to Shapley values to account for subset effects, but such random sampling can struggle to converge and often yields weak explanations in high-dimensional feature spaces [234], which are common in single-cell multi-omic settings.

In this work, we propose GRIDS, a global explanation framework for efficient regulatory redundancy dissection using single-cell multi-modal data. GRIDS consists of two components: a differentiable cross-modality surrogate mapping and a global explanation method based on learnable subset perturbations. To make redundancy analysis tractable in high-dimensional CRE space, the surrogate mapping first learns modality-specific cell representations and aligns them into a shared semantic space through adversarial training, providing a differentiable proxy for the underlying regulatory mapping. Building on this surrogate, GRIDS introduces a learnable subset perturbation procedure with a state transition model to identify a subset of globally important CRE features whose perturbation maximally modifies a target gene's cell-type-specific expression. While this approach belongs to the broader class of perturbation/removal-based explanation methods, it differs from additive or sampling-based subset attribution: GRIDS implements perturbations as discrete replacement operations on the CRE input, while still leveraging standard auto-differentiation to update the subset efficiently by treating the optimization dynamics as if they were continuous. This design enables precise and scalable global feature-importance explanations for large biological datasets.

We evaluate GRIDS against a range of baseline models on image classification benchmarks and single-cell multi-modal datasets. The results show that GRIDS produces more semantically meaningful feature-importance estimates, enabling effective analysis of regulatory redundancy across extensive genomic regions. To our knowledge, this study is the first to integrate global feature explanations with regulatory redundancy analysis in the context of singlecell multi-modal data. The source code is available at https: //github.com/jhliu17/nnpert.

### 5.2 Related Works

A number of single-cell computational frameworks have been proposed to connect CREs with their putative target genes from chromatin accessibility data. Cicero [204] models these links using ATAC-seq profiles through a graphical lasso formulation. ArchR [103] subsequently popularized correlation-based strategies, typically evaluating peak-gene associations in a largely pairwise manner. More recently, DirectNet [293] adopts a predictive viewpoint by fitting a model to infer gene expression from CRE states and then extracting peak-to-gene relationships through model selection. While these approaches have been effective for mapping candidate regulatory connections, they primarily quantify the influence of individual CREs (or simple pairwise relationships). As a result, a central question remains difficult to address: under a perturbation, which set of CREs can act together to maximally alter a target gene's expression?

This gap is closely related to the broader literature on feature-importance explanations for black-box models. Local explanation methods, including LIME [212] and SHAP [169], are designed to attribute importance for individual predictions on a per-instance basis. Com- plementary directions have explored differentiable surrogate models to facilitate explanation generation for complex predictors, as exemplified by Chapman-Rounds et al. [46] and Chen et al. [50]. In contrast, global explanation methods aim to summarize feature effects over a population by assessing performance changes under feature perturbations or by approximating Shapley-style subset contributions [228, 169, 63]. However, despite these advances, producing reliable and efficient explanations remains challenging in extremely high-dimensional settings, where subset interactions are combinatorial and naive sampling or perturbation schemes can become ineffective.

### 5.3 Methods

#### 5.3.1 Problem Setup

Following the formulation in Wu et al. [273], we represent the cis-regulatory landscape of a cell by an ATAC-seq vector x ∈ { 0 , 1 } d a , where each entry corresponds to the accessibility state of a genomic peak (1 for open and 0 for closed). In practice, ATAC-seq profiles are extremely high-dimensional, typically with d a &gt; 10 5 . The corresponding gene expression measurements (RNA-seq) are denoted by a real-valued vector y ∈ R d r , where d a and d r indicate the numbers of peaks and genes, respectively.

Asingle-cell multi-omics dataset is given as a collection of N paired multi-modal observations C = { c (1) , c (2) , . . . , c ( N ) } , where each cell c ( i ) = ( x ( i ) , y ( i ) ) consists of an ATAC-seq profile x ( i ) and its matched RNA-seq profile y ( i ). In addition, each cell c ( i ) is associated with a semantic label ℓ ( i ) ∈ { 1 , . . . , T } indicating its cell type among T classes. For a given cell type T = k , we denote by C k the subset of C containing all cells whose labels satisfy ℓ ( i ) = k .

As introduced above, gene expression is regulated through CRE-mediated transcriptional control implemented by complex intracellular processes. We abstract this relationship using a regulatory function y = F ( x ), where F ( x ) : R d a → R d r . Because F corresponds to an underlying biological mechanism, it is costly to query directly and is treated as a black box. The regulatory redundancy dissection problem then aims to identify a subset of L peak indices r = { r 1 , . . . , r L } within the CRE space (i.e., the subset feature in the ATAC-seq x r ≡ { x j | j ∈ r } ) that are crucial for controlling a target gene's expression over a population of cells. The search domain of r is the binomial subset space ( d a L ) , which is prohibitively large given that x typically contains more than 10 5 dimensions.

To make this problem tractable, we model redundancy dissection within a global feature explanation framework. In general, global explanations quantify how much a model's performance degrades over a sample population when selected features are removed [46]. Under the regulatory redundancy setting, the corresponding global objective can be written as

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where L measures the expected degradation in gene expression. Here, x \ r denotes the perturbed CRE representation induced by r , obtained by replacing the original features x r j with preset perturbation values p ∈ R d a at the indices specified by r (i.e., x \ r,r j = p r j ). When p = 0, this reduces to a removal-based perturbation. The specific form of the loss depends on the model and task [63]; for instance, cross-entropy is commonly used to measure probability degradation in classification, whereas in our setting we adopt a mean-squared loss to quantify degradation of gene expression values. The optimal subset r ∗ in Eq. 1 corresponds to the solution of the regulatory redundancy problem described above.

In practice, directly optimizing Eq. 1 is infeasible because the regulatory function F is a black box and cannot be queried efficiently, which also makes many model-agnostic explanation procedures intractable. We therefore introduce a surrogate model ˆ F ( x ; θ f ) : R d a → R d r , implemented as a neural network trained to serve as a differentiable approximation of F using the collected single-cell multi-modal dataset C . Replacing F with ˆ F ( x ; θ f ) in Eq. 1 yields the tractable objective

Figure 5.1: Overview of our proposed GRIDS method. It comprises two steps: training a crossmodality surrogate model and using a global explanation method to dissect regulatory redundancy. The left panel visualizes the learned ATAC-RNA joint embeddings and the heatmap of GAD1 gene expression.

<!-- image -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

We describe the surrogate construction and training procedure in Section 5.3.2. Nonetheless, even after introducing a differentiable surrogate, existing global explanation methods remain inefficient in large feature spaces and are often impractical for high-dimensional ATAC-seq data. In particular, prior approaches either approximate combinatorial subset effects using additive importance measures that ignore interactions among features [228, 169], or rely on random sampling strategies to estimate subset contributions [63]. We present our approach for addressing this challenge in Section 5.3.3-5.3.4.

#### 5.3.2 Cross-Modality Surrogate Modeling

Given the paired single-cell multi-modal dataset C , our first step is to learn a differentiable surrogate ˆ F that captures the mapping from chromatin accessibility to gene expression. We construct this surrogate by embedding both RNA and ATAC measurements into a shared latent space E . Operating in E not only provides a compact representation for highdimensional inputs, but also offers a convenient interface for extending a surrogate trained on paired RNA-ATAC observations (with known paired cell-type labels) to scenarios involving unpaired data where cell-type annotations may be incomplete or unavailable. To model modality-specific structure before alignment, we employ two autoencoders, one per modality. For ATAC-seq, each coordinate of x is treated as a binary categorical variable, and we introduce a low-dimensional embedding for each category. #### 5.3.3 Regulatory Redundancy as a Global Perturbation Objective

Global explanation objective. Given a differentiable surrogate ˆ F , we quantify how a perturbation subset r impacts expression by measuring the expected degradation induced by removing the corresponding CRE features. Throughout this section we adopt a removal-style perturbation with p = 0, and define the degradation loss as

̸

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where i denotes the target gene, d r is the number of genes, and β controls how strongly the learned perturbation is encouraged to minimize collateral changes on non-target genes. This objective directly instantiates the multi-CRE-to-gene redundancy goal described in Section 5.3.1 by favoring subsets that maximally suppress the target gene while keeping the rest of the transcriptome comparatively stable.

Adirect search over all candidate subsets r ∈ ( d a L ) is infeasible at ATAC scale (e.g., d a &gt; 10 5 ). Since ˆ F is differentiable, we instead freeze the surrogate parameters and use gradients of the tractable objective in Eq. 2 to guide the search for r .

Learning an optimal subset r ∗ . We now revisit the replacement-style perturbation introduced in Section 5.3.1. For a subset r , the perturbed features satisfy x \ r,r j = p r j for each r j ∈ r . While ˆ F is differentiable, the replacement operation itself is discrete, so the subset indices cannot be optimized directly as continuous variables using standard gradient descent. To handle this, we rewrite replacement under an arbitrary perturbation vector p in a unified form:

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where feature j is replaced by p j when j ∈ r and otherwise remains unchanged. This representation makes it explicit how any replacement-based perturbation, including feature removal ( p = 0) or alternative fill-in strategies such as mean imputationm, alters the input. Starting from a randomly initialized subset r , we then compute the gradient of the global objective with respect to the ATAC category embeddings (or directly with respect to the perturbed input if the features are continuous) using standard automatic differentiation:

where G ∈ R d a × d h and the row G j corresponds to feature j in the embedding space.

#### 5.3.4 Learnable Subset Perturbations for Efficient Combinatorial Search

Indices transition estimation. Starting from the gradient signal G , we derive a lightweight proxy that estimates how advantageous it is to perturb each candidate index. Concretely, we compute an index-wise descent score in the embedding space and assemble a transition matrix that compares candidate replacements against the current subset members:

̸

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where ⟨· , ·⟩ denotes the inner product. The matrix T serves as an index transition matrix: T i,j scores the relative advantage of substituting the current element r i with a candidate index j , while accounting for whether j is already selected and what would be removed.

This replacement-based formulation also generalizes to continuous-input modalities (e.g., images) without introducing an additional embedding layer. In that case, the objective gradient can be taken directly with respect to the perturbed inputs x \ r , and the descent proxy simplifies to d j = G j × ( p j -x j ), while the advantage definition in T remains the same.

Subset r learning and update strategy. Once the transition matrix T is computed, we can iteratively refine the global subset r using structured updates. One approach performs coordinate descent: for each position i in r , we first identify the topk candidate indices according to the i th row of T , and then explicitly evaluate these candidates by selecting the replacement that yields the largest decrease in L ( ˆ F ( x \ r ′ ) , y ) for the updated subset r ′ . Alternatively, the subset can be updated jointly by treating index selection as a sequencegeneration problem and applying beam search, which maintains the best partial subsets up to a specified beam size at each step. Empirically, coordinate descent provides a strong balance between runtime and explanation quality, and a randomly initialized subset r can be refined in mini-batches to converge to an optimal subset r ∗ .

Overall, our procedure learns the combinatorial global subset r ∗ through guidance by gradient rather than random subset sampling [63], leading to substantially improved efficiency and more reliable optimization in high-dimensional space (e.g., ATAC-seq with d a &gt; 10 4 ). The complete algorithm is summarized in Algorithm 1 (Appendix D.1).

#### 5.3.5 Evaluation Metrics

Single-Cell Multimodal Dataset. We curated a deeply sequenced single-cell multimodal dataset from postmortem human PFC [79, 5]. The dataset contains N = 10 , 266 cells spanning T = 8 cell types, with each cell profiled for both chromatin accessibility (ATAC-seq) and transcriptional activity (RNA-seq). On the ATAC-seq modality, we called d a = 127 , 219 peaks using Macs2 [296], and the average sequencing depth (i.e., the number of open states per cell) is 4811 . 34. For RNA-seq, we applied standard quality control and preprocessing using the default parameters recommended by Pegasus [154], and retained d r = 3000 genes. Additional dataset details are provided in Appendix D.2. We evaluate GRIDS by learning global feature subsets r of varying sequence length L for multiple target genes, using masking-based perturbations on the CRE input with p = 0. In each experiment, we randomly split the full dataset into training, validation, and test sets with ratios 0 . 7, 0 . 1, and 0 . 2, respectively. Global explanations are learned on the training set and evaluated on the held-out test set. Hyperparameter settings are reported in Appendix D.3.

MNIST Dataset. To further demonstrate the ability of GRIDS to recover combinatorial feature effects, we performed additional experiments on binary digit classification using MNIST. We compared GRIDS with strong baselines under the same global explanation evaluation protocol. The full set of results is summarized in Appendix D.4, where we observe that GRIDS can effectively identify combinatorial features in the input space.

Baseline Comparisons. We compare GRIDS with a collection of local and global featureimportance methods: (1) Random , a naive baseline that selects features uniformly at random for perturbation. (2) Saliency [230], a widely used gradient-based attribution method; we aggregate instance-wise saliency scores across samples to obtain global importance estimates. (3) LIME [212], a local explanation method that uses the submodular pick procedure to derive global feature importance from local explanations. (4) SmoothGrad [232], which samples noisy neighbors and estimates importance using averaged gradient-based saliency maps. (5) FIMAP [46], a neural-network-based approach that learns feature importance by finding minimal adversarial perturbations. (6) CXPlain [228], a global method that trains a surrogate explainer and perturbs features with predefined values to infer their importance. (7) SAGE [63], which extends SHAP [169] to provide global explanations via sampling-based approximations to Shapley values over feature subsets.

### 5.4 Results

#### 5.4.1 Surrogate translates accurately to support perturbation-based reasoning

As GRIDS relies on a differentiable surrogate ˆ F to approximate the translation from ATAC to RNA, we first assessed surrogate accuracy on the single-cell multimodal dataset by selecting a curated panel of marker genes from Lake et al. [148] and comparing mean expression profiles across cell types between the observed cohort and the translated outputs; these marker genes serve as highly discriminative indicators of cell identity and are consistent with the category labels used in the downstream classification setting. We observed that GRIDS preserves cell-type-specific expression structure closely matching the ground truth (mean R 2 =0.914). We then examined four canonical markers: SATB2 (excitatory), GAD2 (inhibitory), FLT1 (endothelial), and MOG (oligodendrocyte). From the markers, we found that their UMAP patterns agree with expected cell-type localization, while the translated expressions remain well correlated with observed values ( R 2 =0.633, 0.603, 0.460, 0.684, respectively). Together, these results indicate that ˆ F provides an accurate differentiable approximation of the blackbox ATAC-to-RNA mapping, supporting downstream perturbation-based analysis.

#### 5.4.2 Learnable global subset perturbations outperform baselines in suppressing target gene expression

We next evaluated GRIDS for dissecting the regulatory redundancy from multiple CREs to one single gene by producing global feature-importance explanations in high-throughput single-cell multi-omics data.

###### Cross-cell-type perturbation patterns reveal redundancy heterogeneity and shared regulatory programs

A central difficulty in single-cell regulatory analysis is the extreme dimensionality of the feature space; for example, ATAC-seq profiles routinely contain almost millions of peaks, making classical leave-one-out strategies impractical [156]. GRIDS addresses this challenge by learning global feature-importance explanations that efficiently identify influential CRE subsets. To evaluate both effectiveness and robustness, we benchmarked GRIDS against multiple baselines in two complementary settings: (1) a gene-focused benchmark using marker genes from seven cell types, and (2) a cell-type-focused benchmark using comprehensive sets of highly expressed genes from two representative cell types (VIP and Microglia); the complete marker lists are provided in Appendix D.2.1. We quantify how well each method suppresses a target gene by masking L CRE features using two metrics: the averaged expression change (Avg. ∆) and the relative expression change with respect to the unperturbed value (Rel. ∆). Benchmarking results for the gene-focused and cell-type-focused settings are summarized in Table 5.1 and Table 5.2, respectively. Although LIME, CXPlain, and SAGE perform well on MNIST (Appendix D.4), all three fail to produce useful global explanations for high-dimensional ATAC-seq data in our experiments. In particular, due to the curse of dimensionality, LIME and CXPlain struggle to generate reasonable explanations in ATAC-seq, suggesting that simple surrogate models (e.g., K-Lasso in LIME) or regular masking schemes (e.g., sliding windows in CXPlain) are insufficient to capture the relevant feature effects in such vast spaces. Meanwhile, SAGE does not converge within a practical time budget, as

it relies on randomly sampling subsets from an enormous combinatorial space to estimate expected degradation; this importance-sampling-style strategy is prone to high variance and weight degeneracy in high dimensions. As shown in Table 5.1, in the gene-focused benchmark GRIDS consistently produces larger marker-gene expression degradation than all baselines across cell types, with an average change of -7 . 30% compared to -0 . 02% to -5 . 36% for other methods ( p -value &lt; 0.01, one-sided t-test). We further repeated the evaluation with a larger perturbation budget ( L = 128), observing the same overall trends, which supports the effectiveness of the GRIDS subset search. In the cell-type-focused benchmark (Table 5.2), we evaluated expression changes for the top 100 highly expressed genes in VIP and Microglia after masking L CRE inputs selected by each method, and found that Random, Saliency, and SmoothGrad rarely identify subsets that yield substantial suppression.

We hypothesize that the baselines did not perform well because the mapping relationship between RNA and ATAC is computationally complicated and may require multi-step reasoning over feature interactions; consequently, one-step gradient-based signals can be unreliable if they are not coupled with explicit CRE removal evaluations. On the other hand, both FIMAP and GRIDS are able to substantially suppress target-gene expression while masking only 10 CRE features. Moreover, the global feature importance produced by GRIDS yields noticeably larger expression shifts than FIMAP, supporting its effectiveness and robustness for dissecting regulatory redundancy. We further found cell-type-specific marker-gene expression changes after removing L = 128 CREs selected across all cell types in Figure 5.2. As expected, the largest expression decreases occur when applying explanations learned from the matched cell type (i.e., the diagonal). At the same time, perturbing CRE subsets learned from closely related cell types can still induce meaningful expression changes, consistent with shared regulatory programs. For example, SST and VIP are inhibitory-neuron subtypes that share the pan-inhibitory marker gene GAD1 , and we observe a slightly reduced yet still substantial expression decrease in SST cells when masking CRE sets selected from VIP cells. Overall, these results suggest that GRIDS can identify cell-type-specific optimal CRE subsets, enabling characterization of regulatory redundancy heterogeneity across diverse cellular populations.

###### The discovered CRE subsets are biologically plausible and supported by orthogonal evidence

To assess whether the global explanations produced by GRIDS and other baselines are informative for downstream biological interpretation, we evaluated the reported CRE subsets using a distance-based proxy for CRE-gene association. Because genomic distance is a major determinant of CRE-gene interactions, we defined a local neighborhood spanning 10 million base pairs (MB) around each target gene, within which direct CRE-gene regulation via chromatin looping is plausible and commonly assumed in genomics [202, 247]. We then quantified how many of the selected L CREs fall within this neighborhood using the Soft Hit Ratio (SHR). In addition, we computed the Hit Ratio (HR) as a stricter criterion that measures exact matches, i.e., whether the reported regions are directly relevant to the target gene under this local-neighborhood definition.

As summarized in Table 5.3, Saliency and SmoothGrad identify only a small number of CREs within the local neighborhood for both L = 10 and L = 128, which is understandable due to their limited ability to suppress expression when masking the corresponding features. In contrast, GRIDS produces global explanations with a substantially higher proportion of locally proximal CREs compared to the FIMAP baseline, supporting the biological plausibility of the discovered subsets. We further performed an independent validation using cell-type-matched Hi-C experiments (Appendix D.5). We note that, in general, expressionguided subset search methods may have difficulty separating direct CRE-gene regulation mediated by physical contact from effects arising through indirect pathways. For example, some CREs may primarily regulate upstream transcription factors (TFs), which can propagate downstream effects to the target gene without requiring direct physical interactions. Incorporating additional side information into the regulatory function could help more faithfully capture such global feature interactions when using model explanation methods.

### 5.5 Discussion

In summary, GRIDS provides a principled route for studying multi-CRE-to-gene regulatory redundancy through the lens of global model explanations in single-cell multi-modal data. The key design choice is to first learn a differentiable cross-modality surrogate that approximates the ATAC-to-RNA regulatory mapping, transforming an otherwise expensive black-box relationship into a tractable objective for perturbation-based analysis. On top of this surrogate, GRIDS introduces a learnable subset-perturbation framework that replaces brute-force or sampling-heavy subset searches with an efficient, gradient-guided procedure for identifying influential CRE sets. This combination enables scalable global explanations in extremely high-dimensional settings, and the same perturbation-learning machinery can be applied beyond ATAC-seq to other modalities, including high-throughput biological sequences.

Empirically, GRIDS outperforms strong baselines on both image benchmarks and singlecell multi-omics experiments, indicating that it produces more effective and semantically meaningful global feature subsets. Finally, the cross-cell-type and regional analyses show that the learned subsets capture cell-type-dependent redundancy structure, offering interpretable, population-level hypotheses that can help prioritize targeted follow-up experiments and guide wet-lab validation efforts.

### Chapter 6

## Disease-Driven Regulatory Rewiring with Multi-Omic and Spatial Validation

### 6.1 Introduction

Post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD) is a prevalent psychiatric condition that can develop following exposure to severe traumatic events. In the general population, PTSD affects roughly 6-8% of individuals [183, 30]. Genetic studies indicate moderate heritability (approximately 24%-40%) [3], and large-scale association analyses support a highly polygenic architecture [237, 77, 96, 196]. Recent post-mortem investigations have started to clarify the molecular features of brains from individuals with PTSD [99, 166, 122, 69]. Work to date has largely centered on the prefrontal cortex (PFC) and amygdala, revealing alterations across multiple pathways, including GABAergic signalling, immunity and neuroinflammation, and glucocorticoid signalling. The breadth of implicated biological processes suggests that PTSD pathophysiology is unlikely to be attributable to a single cell type.

In the human brain, transcriptional programs are shaped by cis-regulatory elements (CREs) together with chromatin modifications and transcription factor binding at enhancers and promoters. Establishing which CREs are active in specific cell types is essential for understanding how disease-associated variants influence gene expression. Prior studies have reported robust PTSD-associated differences in DNA methylation [69, 155]; however, to our knowledge, the single-cell chromatin landscape of the brain has not been comprehensively characterized in PTSD. Advances in genomic profiling now make it possible to measure chromatin organization at single-cell resolution, and to pair these measurements with gene expression to pinpoint how risk variants modulate transcription within individual cellular contexts [219, 151, 185].

Here, we report a multi-omic study of over two million nuclei isolated from the dorsolateral PFC (DLPFC) spanning three diagnostic groups: individuals with major depressive disorder (MDD), individuals with PTSD, and control individuals with neither condition (CON). Using paired single-nucleus RNA sequencing (snRNA-seq) and single-nucleus assay for transposase-accessible chromatin sequencing (snATAC-seq), we integrated transcriptomic and epigenomic profiles to annotate and quantify all major brain cell classes, including excitatory and inhibitory neurons as well as non-neuronal populations. This atlas enabled detection of cell-type-specific differentially expressed genes (DEGs) in PTSD and identification of both shared and distinct expression changes between PTSD and MDD. We additionally performed high-resolution single-cell spatial transcriptomics on tissue from 18 donors, which corroborated DEGs and revealed disrupted cell-to-cell communication (CCC) patterns involving somatostatin (SST) interneurons and microglia in PTSD. To link regulatory variation to transcriptional outcomes, we integrated RNA and ATAC modalities to define CREs, connect them to putative target genes, and fine-map eight credible risk loci within these candidate enhancers. We uncovered selective perturbations of the glucocorticoid system that were, unexpectedly, most prominent in endothelial cells. We further observed heightened vulnerability of SST interneurons in PTSD, alongside transcriptome-wide shifts consistent with reduced SST signalling and neurotransmission. Collectively, these results delineate pathway-level dynamics across diverse cortical cell types and provide predictions of cis-regulatory logic and associated factors that contribute to the molecular aetiology of PTSD.

### 6.2 Single-cell multi-omic analysis of the PFC in PTSD

To characterize cell-type-resolved transcriptional and regulatory alterations in PTSD (Fig. 6.1a), we generated snRNA-seq and snATAC-seq profiles from dorsolateral PFC (DLPFC) tissue collected from 111 donors. The cohort comprised 36 donors with PTSD, 36 donors with major depressive disorder (MDD; psychiatric control), and 39 control donors with neither diagnosis (CON) (Extended Data Fig. 1a). We present findings from a discovery dataset including 935,371 nuclei assayed by snRNA-seq (Fig. 6.1b and Supplementary Fig. 1), 473,033 nuclei assayed by snATAC-seq (Fig. 6.1c and Supplementary Fig. 2), and 119,431 nuclei profiled by single-nucleus multiome (snMultiome) (Fig. 6.1d and Supplementary Fig. 3). The snMultiome component provided direct pairing of barcoded mRNA and chromatin accessibility from the same nuclei. Four snMultiome samples overlapped with the discovery cohort (Extended Data Fig. 1b and Supplementary Table 1).

Across snRNA-seq, we annotated major cortical cell classes using established marker-gene expression (Extended Data Fig. 2d), including excitatory neurons (EXNs; four subtypes: CUX2, RORB, FEZF2, and OPRK1), inhibitory neurons (INs; five subtypes: LAMP5, KCNG1, VIP, SST, and PVALB), oligodendrocytes (OLGs), oligodendrocyte progenitor cells (OPCs), endothelial cells (ENDs), astrocytes (ASTs), and microglia (MG) (Fig. 6.1b and Extended Data Fig. 2a). Nuclei counts by cell type and modality are summarized in Supplementary Table 2. We quantified differential cell-type abundance using scCODA [36] based on nuclei numbers across modalities, and observed reduced OLG abundance (false discovery rate (FDR) &lt; 0.05) in PTSD compared with both CON and MDD (Fig. 6.1b). Using snATAC-seq, we recovered the same seven canonical brain cell types (Fig. 6.1c and Extended Data Fig. 2b), supported by cell-type-specific accessibility at marker loci (Extended Data Fig. 2e), and obtained concordant annotations in snMultiome (Fig. 6.1d and Extended Data Fig. 2c). To resolve finer transcriptomic structure (Extended Data Fig. 2f), we leveraged prior subtype annotations [172] and identified 61 subtypes comprising 18 EXN subtypes, 30 IN subtypes, and 13 non-neuronal subtypes, including two OLG subtypes, three AST subtypes, macrophages, T cells (T SKAP1 CD247), smooth muscle cells, and pericytes (Extended Data Fig. 2f,g).

### 6.3 PTSD-specific changes in gene expression

To identify PTSD-associated transcriptional differences, we tested differential expression across all 14 cell-type clusters using multiple complementary approaches. We first applied two widely used cell-level DEG methods, MAST [82] (Supplementary Table 3) and a Wilcoxon rank-sum test (Supplementary Table 4). To further account for sample-level effects, we additionally used a mixed linear model framework (nebula) [109] (Supplementary Table 5). Finally, we performed a sample-level pseudobulk analysis with DESeq2 [167] (Supplementary Table 6). Throughout, we define snDEGs as genes that are significant in both MAST and Wilcoxon with concordant directionality (Methods). Differential expression was assessed for both canonical cell types and finer subtypes (Fig. 6.1e). In neurons, we detected 322 DEGs in excitatory neurons (EXNs) and 66 DEGs in inhibitory neurons (INs), with the largest number of changes observed in CUX2 EXNs (557). Considering the seven major cell types, MASTidentified 3,431 DEGs and Wilcoxon identified 2,989 DEGs; among these, 1,376 DEGs overlapped with the same direction of effect, corresponding to 1,184 unique genes (PTSD snDEGs; Fig. 6.1f and Supplementary Table 7). Only 9.92% of DEGs were significantly altered in both EXNs and INs (Fig. 6.1f), and we did not observe any single DEG shared across all cell types. Cosine-similarity analysis indicated that expression changes were most concordant among neuronal subtypes (Extended Data Fig. 3a).

Functional enrichment analyses of PTSD snDEGs (Extended Data Fig. 3b,c and Supplementary Table 8) and of cell-type-specific DEGs (Extended Data Fig. 3d and Supplementary Table 9) highlighted pathways related to ubiquitin binding, cellular stress responses, and cadherin binding. Results from nebula were consistent and additionally implicated cadherin binding, phosphodiesterase signaling, and axonogenesis (Supplementary Table 10) across 437 DEGs. In the pseudobulk framework, additional significant DEGs yielded enrichment for transcription factor binding and estrogen receptor signaling, driven primarily by EXNs (73) and INs (19), with comparatively few findings in non-neuronal populations (for example, MGs (8) and OPC (1); Supplementary Table 11). We also observed sex-specific transcriptional responses in PTSD and MDD, in line with prior reports [99] (Supplementary Tables 12-16).

We next evaluated concordance with bulk RNA-seq from high-quality tissue in a partially overlapping cohort (n of approximately 150 samples)9 and found that the leading PTSD snDEGs showed consistent effect directions and similar magnitudes (Fig. 6.1g), including previously reported genes such as FKBP5 and YBX3 [99] and HDAC9 [122]. At a genomewide level, effect sizes were strongly correlated between bulk and single-nucleus analyses (r=0.69; Extended Data Fig. 3e). Our subtype-resolved analysis also revealed candidate genes with more specific patterns, including GRM5 [112, 71] in LAMP5 INs and CLU [275] in CUX2 and OPRK1 EXNs (Supplementary Table 7), which have been linked previously to suicide risk and Alzheimer's disease, respectively. Notably, FKBP5 expression was increased in ENDs (log2 fold change (log2FC)=1.42, FDR=3.42 × 10 -128 ) and in OLGs (log2FC=1.02, FDR=7.63 × 10 -282 ; Fig. 6.1g). FKBP5 has been reported to be upregulated in post-mortem frontal cortex in PTSD9 and to be inversely associated with mushroom spine density [285]. Finally, comparison to a smaller, partially overlapping dataset (n=10-11 per diagnostic group) [47] showed moderate agreement in both the number and direction of nominally sig- nificant DEGs for our PTSD (61.4%) and MDD (72.7%) cohorts across cell types (Extended Data Fig. 3f,g).

### 6.4 Gene expression comparisons between PTSD and MDD

Because major depressive disorder (MDD) is frequently comorbid among individuals diagnosed with PTSD (reported in more than 50% of cases) [29, 137, 216], we included an MDD group as a psychiatric control to help separate shared from PTSD-specific molecular signals. We compared genes upregulated and downregulated in PTSD versus CON with those altered in MDD versus CON to delineate overlapping and distinct mechanisms. The MDD cohort was processed and analyzed using the same pipeline as the PTSD cohort (Methods), yielding 1,918 MDD snDEGs (Extended Data Fig. 4a and Supplementary Table 17). Across conditions, snDEGs showed substantial overlap: 57.6% of snDEGs were shared between MDD and PTSD (Fig. 6.1h), while 502 snDEGs were specific to PTSD and 1,236 were specific to MDD (Supplementary Tables 18 and 19). Pathway analysis of PTSD-specific snDEGs indicated enrichment for calmodulin signaling, cadherins, and ubiquitin binding (Fig. 6.1i and Supplementary Table 20). For MDD, global transcript-level effects were highly concordant between snRNA-seq and bulk datasets (r=0.72; Extended Data Fig. 4b), with moderate agreement among the strongest MDD DEGs (Extended Data Fig. 4c). The greatest DEG overlap between MDD and PTSD occurred in non-neuronal populations (Extended Data Fig. 4d).

To more directly assess differences between disorders, we focused on genes showing divergent regulation (that is, increased in PTSD but decreased in MDD, or the converse) using a threshold-free rank-rank hypergeometric overlap framework [37]. This analysis revealed strong enrichment for concordant signals (87.8%) and comparatively weak enrichment for discordant signals, assessed both by P value (Extended Data Fig. 4e) and odds ratio (Extended Data Fig. 4f). Across cell types, 12 genes exhibited significant discordant regulation (down in PTSD and up in MDD). Notably, CTNNA3 (encoding catenin α 3) was decreased in seven of nine neuronal subtypes in PTSD but increased in MDD, and HSPA1A (encoding heat shock protein A1A) was reduced in PTSD yet elevated in endothelial cells in MDD (Fig. 6.1j and Supplementary Table 7).

The cohort size also enabled a targeted analysis of donors diagnosed with PTSD in the absence of depression. We computed DEGs for PTSD without depression (n=8; Supplementary Table 21) and for PTSD comorbid with depression (n=27; Supplementary Table 22), each contrasted against CON, and then assessed their overlap with the primary PTSD snDEGs. For most cell types, PTSD with comorbid depression showed the largest DEG overlap with the overall PTSD signature (Extended Data Fig. 4g). Although the PTSD-only subgroup is underpowered relative to the full study, several signals were nonetheless apparent. In EXNs, we observed significant changes in BDNF (log2FC= -0.45, FDR=3.86 × 10 -40 ), a gene long linked to the actions of classical [195] and rapid-acting antidepressants [45] (Supplementary Table 21). While these results should be interpreted cautiously, this preliminary comparison underscores the need for larger post-mortem collections of PTSD donors without comorbid mood disorders to more cleanly resolve PTSD-specific molecular effects.

### 6.5 Spatial validation of PTSD gene expression changes

Our snRNA-seq analyses revealed widespread transcriptomic alterations across DLPFC cell types. To independently validate these signals in situ, we generated single-cell-resolution spatial expression profiles using the 10X Xenium platform for 18 samples (4 PTSD, 4 MDD, and 10 CON; Supplementary Fig. 4). We designed a targeted 366-gene panel, chosen to include canonical cell-type markers and genes showing differential expression in our snRNAseq results (Fig. 6.2a, Extended Data Fig. 1c, and Supplementary Table 23). Spatial data were processed using both soma-based parsing (Supplementary Table 24) and nucleus-based parsing (Supplementary Table 25). We then clustered transcripts with Squidpy [197] and assigned canonical cell-type labels by leveraging annotations derived from the snRNA-seq atlas (Fig. 6.2a,b). In total, we detected 550,520 cells in the soma-parsed dataset (scXenium), with a median of 347 total transcripts and 100 unique transcripts per cell (Extended Data Fig. 5a, top). The nucleus-parsed dataset (snXenium) comprised 523,314 nuclei, with a median of 87 total transcripts and 42 unique transcripts per nucleus (Extended Data Fig. 5a, bottom). Spatial marker distributions recapitulated expected cortical architecture, including enrichment of END markers (consistent with blood vessels) near the pial surface and localization of OLG markers to the white matter (Extended Data Fig. 5b). Cell-type-resolved maps and transcript-level visualization further captured fine anatomical structure in the DLPFC at high resolution (Extended Data Fig. 5c, top). Post-Xenium haematoxylin and eosin staining did not reveal major anatomical differences across samples (Extended Data Fig. 5c, bottom).

To assess concordance with snRNA-seq, we tested case-control expression differences in the Xenium data (n=4 MDD and n=4 PTSD versus n=10 CON) across DLPFC cell types using MAST (Fig. 6.2c). Most differential signals were observed in neuronal populations. Comparing Xenium-derived effects to the snRNA-seq MAST results, we observed the greatest agreement in fold-change direction for neurons (73% in EXNs and 42% in INs), as well as for OPCs (64%) and ENDs (62%), yielding an overall directional overlap of 53% (Fig. 6.2d). At the transcriptome-wide level, nuclear measurements were strongly correlated between snRNA-seq and snXenium (r=0.62; Extended Data Fig. 5d), and similarly between snRNAseq and scXenium (r=0.61; Extended Data Fig. 5e). The comparable correlations for snXenium and scXenium indicate that soma-level signals are well captured by nuclear transcript measurements.

Within endothelial cells, we detected 88 significant DEGs in the Xenium analysis (Fig. 6.2e), including 31 that overlapped with snRNA-seq END DEGs. Consistent with our single- nucleus findings, FKBP5 exhibited high expression in PTSD non-neuronal compartments and showed a significant increase in ENDs (log2FC=0.14, FDR &lt; 0.01; Fig. 6.2f). Spatially, FKBP5 signal was broadly distributed across the DLPFC (Fig. 6.2g,h) and was notably elevated in regions adjacent to blood vessels (Fig. 6.2g,h). We also observed substantial alterations in inhibitory neurons, identifying 102 DEGs with a moderate overlap (42%) with snRNA DEGs (Extended Data Fig. 5f). Among the most upregulated transcripts was CXCL14 (log2FC=1.4, FDR &lt; 0.01; Extended Data Fig. 5g-i), a chemokine expressed in interneurons across the brain that has been reported to suppress GABAergic transmission [159, 17].

### 6.6 PTSD alters interneuron and microglia communication

To interpret the functional consequences of PTSD-associated transcriptional shifts, we performed a cell-cell communication (CCC) analysis using curated ligand-receptor pairs and constructed a ligand-receptor interaction network based on transcript abundance (Methods). This analysis revealed reduced microglial sender activity in PTSD, whereas microglial sending patterns were increased in MDD (Extended Data Fig. 6a). The contrast was primarily attributable to elevated SPP1 expression in microglia together with neuronal expression of integrin receptors within the SPP1 (osteopontin) pathway (Extended Data Fig. 6b). Notably, the osteopontin receptor subunit integrin α 4 exhibited differential regulation across multiple neuronal subtypes, with increased expression in PTSD but decreased expression in MDD (Supplementary Table 26).

We also observed evidence for altered neuronal communication in PTSD, consistent with disruptions in synaptic transmission. Because neurotransmitter concentrations are not directly measurable from gene expression, we used NeuronChat [297] to infer neurotransmitter signaling by leveraging expression of synthesis and transport transcripts. We quantified differences in sender signaling between PTSD and CON. In PTSD, somatostatin (SST) inhibitory neurons (INs) showed a pronounced reduction in outgoing communication relative to other neuronal populations (Fig. 6.3a), consistent with previous findings [99]. Moreover, communication strength from SST INs to each other neuronal class was reduced in PTSD, with additional decreases in interactions involving ASTs, ENDs, and OPCs (Fig. 6.3b and Supplementary Table 27). In parallel, SST itself was significantly downregulated in INs (Fig. 6.3c) across the DLPFC (Fig. 6.3d,e). The strongest reduction in inferred communication was observed from SST INs to KCNG1 INs. Relative to other inhibitory populations, SST INs appeared to underutilize GABA, driven in part by reduced expression of the vesicular GABA transporter transcript SLC32A1 (CON third quartile expression=0.196 TPM (transcripts per million); PTSD third quartile expression=0 TPM), which in turn corresponded to diminished predicted GABA output onto GABRA5 , GABBR1 , GABRB1 , and GABRG1 receptors (Fig. 6.3f).

The SST IN was downregulated in its output signalling (log differential output = -0.518). b, Circos plot showing differential strength of all cell-to-cell interactions from SST INs between PTSD and CON. The shading indicates their quantitative values, with higher numbers indicating higher differential strengths. c, Mean SST log-normalized counts comparing 10 CON versus 4 PTSD samples by cell types. d, CON slide with SST transcripts indicated in dark green and each nucleus in its corresponding cell-type colour in a lighter shade (IN in light green). The CON inset zooms into layer 5, showing higher expression of SST in IN nuclei. One experiment was conducted. e, PTSD slide with SST transcripts indicated in dark green and each nucleus in its corresponding cell-type colour in a lighter shade (IN in light green). The PTSD inset zooms into layer 5, where SST expression is decreased. One experiment was conducted. f, Circos plots of GABA-GABRA5, GABA-GABBR1, GABA-GABRB1 and GABA-GABRG1 showing a decrease in differential output strength of all cell-to-cell interactions from SST INs in individuals with PTSD. Edges are coloured by interaction strength in PTSD cells (red denotes stronger, and blue indicates weaker). g, Schematic of the slice electrophysiology experimental setup. ChR2 was selectively expressed in SST IN in the mouse mPFC. IPSCs were evoked with 470-nm light pulses and recorded in mPFC layer 5 pyramidal neurons (Pyr). h, Representative eIPSCs (baseline in black, MRX 016 in red, and after washout in grey) recorded in control versus SPS mice i, Quantification of the percentage of IPSC amplitude change in the GABAR α 5 antagonist MRX 016 compared with baseline. Two-sided Student's t-test was used, P = 0.01. n = 10 control samples and n = 18 SPS samples. Lines indicate mean, and error bars denote s.e.m. j, Schematic of the experimental setup. k, Representative traces from control (top) and SPS (bottom) held at 0 mV with Baclofen (a GABAB receptor agonist) and washing in CGP 55845 (a GABAB receptor antagonist). CGP 55845 induced a decrease in GABA tonic current in a pyramidal neuron in control but not in SPS. Red dotted lines indicate average baseline holding current before and after CGP 55845 bath application. l, Quantification of the size of GABA tonic current in control versus SPS. Two-sided Welch's t-test was used, P = 0.02. n = 28 control samples and n = 22 SPS samples. Lines indicate mean, and error bars denote s.e.m. *P &lt; 0.05.

<!-- image -->

To experimentally validate reduced SST IN to EXN communication via GABRA5 , we used the single prolonged stress (SPS) rodent model of traumatic stress [74] (Extended Data Fig. 6c,d) and measured synaptic function in mouse PFC (mPFC) slices with patch-clamp electrophysiology. Channelrhodopsin-2 (ChR2) was selectively expressed in SST INs, and we recorded optically evoked SST IN-mediated inhibitory postsynaptic currents (eIPSCs) in excitatory neurons (EXNs) from control and SPS mice (Fig. 6.3g). Bath application of the GABA receptor α 5 subunit (GABRA α 5)-selective antagonist MRK 016 produced a significant reduction in eIPSC amplitude in control mice (Fig. 6.3h,i), indicating that GABRA α 5-containing receptors contribute substantially to SST IN-to-EXN eIPSCs. In contrast, MRK 016 led to only a small decrease in average amplitude in SPS mice, and this change was significantly smaller than in controls, consistent with reduced GABRA5mediated SST IN → EXN transmission following SPS (Fig. 6.3i). We further quantified tonic GABA current in EXNs from control versus SPS mice (Fig. 6.3j). Tonic inhibition is me- diated by GABAB receptors (containing the GABABR1 subunit; encoded by GABABR1 ) directly and by extrasynaptic GABAA receptors that are regulated by GABAB receptors [61]. SST INs can suppress excitatory transmission through tonic activation of GABAB receptors [259]. Consistent with weakened tonic inhibition after SPS, blocking GABAB receptors with CGP 55845 produced a significantly larger reduction in tonic current in control mice than in SPS mice (Fig. 6.3k,l). Together, these electrophysiological results support reduced SST IN synaptic output onto EXNs in SPS mice.

We next examined neurotransmitter-receptor interactions and focused on the top 15 most differential interactions between PTSD and CON (Extended Data Fig. 6e). We observed a significant, though not complete, reduction in information flow involving Glu-GRM2 and CORT-SSTR2 signaling in PTSD, along with significantly reduced signaling across ten additional pairings spanning both glutamatergic and GABAergic circuits (Extended Data Fig. 6e). In contrast, CRH (encoding corticotropin-releasing hormone)-CRHR1 signaling was increased in PTSD (generally from INs to EXNs; Extended Data Fig. 6f), consistent with CRH involvement in stress-responsive pathways. We highlight several cell-type-resolved ligand-receptor signals, including CRH-CRHR1, Glu-GRM5, CORT-SSTR2, and GluGRM7 (P=0.0039, 1.8 × 10 -12 , 0.06, and 1.8 × 10 -12 , respectively). The strongest increases were observed for CRH-CRHR1 from VIP INs to CUX2 EXNs, for CORT-SSTR2 from LAMP5 INs to OPRK1 EXNs, for Glu-GRM5 from CUX2 EXNs to FEZF2 EXNs, and for Glu-GRM7 from CUX2 EXNs to OPCs (Extended Data Fig. 6f). Collectively, these results indicate that PTSD-associated, cell-type-specific transcriptional changes reshape glutamatergic and GABAergic communication in the cortex and suggest a mechanistic contribution of disrupted neuronal signaling to PTSD pathophysiology.

### 6.7 PTSD alters cis -regulation of gene expression

We used snATAC-seq to profile chromatin accessibility and infer cell-type-resolved cis -regulatory programs associated with PTSD. Across all cell types, we identified 913,717 accessible chromatin peaks, the majority of which mapped to non-coding genomic regions, including introns (51.1%) and intergenic intervals (26.2%) (Fig. 6.4a). Nearly half of these peaks exhibited cell-type specificity (Fig. 6.4b and Extended Data Fig. 7a), with the strongest peakset overlap observed between excitatory and inhibitory neurons (EXNs-INs; 57.1%) and between oligodendrocytes and their progenitors (OLGs-OPCs; 57.2%; Fig. 6.4b and Extended Data Fig. 7b). Comparison with bulk-tissue open chromatin annotations from DLPFC [250] (bCREs) supported these results, as most peaks (88.6%) overlapped (fraction &gt; 0.4) when averaged across the seven major cell types (Extended Data Fig. 7c). By integrating snATACseq with six high-quality snRNA-seq samples, we further inferred 1,433,145 peak-to-gene links using union peaks (correlation &gt; 0.45, FDR &lt; 1 × 10 -4 ; Fig. 6.4c). Because prior work has shown that not all accessible regions directly influence transcription [88], we operationally defined cis-regulatory elements (CREs) as peaks supported by peak-to-gene linkage (correlation &gt; 0.4, FDR &lt; 0.05; Supplementary Table 28). Under this definition, 89.18% of peaks qualified as CREs across cell types (Fig. 6.4d and Supplementary Table 29).

To connect regulatory variation to disease-associated transcriptional shifts, we intersected CRE-linked genes (CLGs) with DEGs within each cell type, yielding CLG-DEG sets. The largest overlaps were observed in endothelial cells (ENDs; 544) and EXNs (313; Fig. 6.4e). In EXNs and ASTs, most CLG-DEGs corresponded to upregulated transcripts (84% in EXNs and 66% in ASTs), and CLG-target DEGs shared across cell types were typically linked to distinct CREs (Extended Data Fig. 7d). For instance, YBX3 is upregulated in both ENDs and ASTs (Fig. 6.1g), yet the associated regulatory links arise from separate, cell-typespecific CREs (Supplementary Table 29). A similar pattern was observed for FKBP5 , which is broadly expressed (Fig. 6.4f) and significantly upregulated in non-neuronal populations
