Peak-to-gene links (n = 1,433,145) are shown using thresholds: FDR &lt; 1 × 10-4 and correlation &gt; 0.45. d, Donut plot showing the number of CREs (n = 395,932) with significantly linked genes (correlation &gt; 0.4 and FDR &lt; 0.05) separated by genomic category. e, Venn diagrams showing the intersection of CLGs and PTSD DEGs for each cell type. f, UMAP plot of FKBP5 highlighted by snRNA-seq log-normalized gene expression (top) and snATAC-seq gene score (bottom). Non-neuronal cell types with high expression of FKBP5 are outlined by the dashed line. g, Chromatin accessibility signal tracks highlighting FKBP5 peak-to-gene links (correlation &gt; 0.75) across cell types in region chromosome 6 (Chr. 6): 35200000-35900000. The signals for non-neuronal cell types are indicated by the dashed boxes.

<!-- image -->

(Fig. 6.1g). Peak-to-gene links with particularly strong correlation to the FKBP5 transcription start site (TSS) (correlation &gt; 0.75) were restricted to END and MG CREs, consistent with cell-type-specific regulatory control of FKBP5 expression (Fig. 6.4g, dotted boxes, and Supplementary Table 29).

To distinguish epigenomic effects associated with PTSD versus MDD, we called peaks separately by condition within each cell type (Supplementary Fig. 5a). After removing peaks shared between conditions and applying the same CRE definition, we identified 141,939 condition-specific CREs across the seven canonical cell types, which we further categorized by genic annotation (Supplementary Table 30 and Supplementary Fig. 5b). Using these PTSDspecific and MDD-specific CRE sets, we derived PTSD CLG-DEGs and MDD CLG-DEGs across cell types (Supplementary Fig. 5c,d). Overlap analysis between the two disorders highlighted disorder-specific regulatory signatures, including 383 PTSD-specific CLG-DEGs and 1,063 MDD-specific CLG-DEGs globally. Notably, CLG-DEGs substantially overlapped with snDEGs for both the PTSD-specific (86%) and MDD-specific (72%) sets (Supplementary Fig. 5e), supporting disease-specific chromatin-linked regulation of transcription.

We further validated these observations using snMultiome data from a subset of samples. Consistent with the snATAC-seq analyses, we inferred peak-to-gene linkages across 14 subtypes in snMultiome (Extended Data Fig. 8a). We identified 774,147 peaks across the seven canonical cell types (Extended Data Fig. 8b) and 1,614,118 peaks across all 14 subtypes (Extended Data Fig. 8c). In line with bulk ATAC-seq comparisons, snMultiome peaks showed approximately 90% overlap with bulk peak sets for both cell types and subtypes at a minimum overlap threshold of 0.4 (Extended Data Fig. 8d,e). Jaccard similarity matrices indicated strong concordance among neuronal peak sets, with particularly high similarity within EXN populations (Extended Data Fig. 8f,g). Finally, peak calls were highly concordant between snATAC-seq and snMultiome across the seven major cell types (Extended Data Fig. 8h).

### 6.8 Transcription factor-binding is altered by PTSD

To probe upstream regulatory drivers of PTSD-associated transcriptional changes, we inferred transcription factor binding motifs [224] within CREs that were linked to DEGs. We first quantified TF motif variability across individual cell clusters (Extended Data Fig. 9a). Motif enrichment patterns showed substantial sharing across cell types, consistent with prior single-nucleus chromatin studies [188]. We posited that PTSD-associated differences in chromatin accessibility capture TF binding sites that regulate PTSD snDEGs. To evaluate this hypothesis, we tested enrichment of binding motifs for the top 20 TFs within cell-typespecific CREs and, for each cell type, identified DEGs connected to these TF-CRE-gene relationships (Supplementary Tables 31-37). Among the top enriched TFs in EXNs, EGR1 was associated with the largest number of regulated DEGs (Supplementary Table 38), and neuronal TF-regulated gene sets displayed extensive overlap across subtypes (Extended Data Fig. 9e). Below, we highlight two TFs that show strong enrichment in EXNs (Extended Data Fig. 9b) and INs (Extended Data Fig. 9f), and that sit upstream of both DEGs and PTSD genetic risk genes.

To examine TF binding dynamics more directly, we performed TF footprinting for two candidate regulators. In EXNs, EGR2 showed stronger motif enrichment in PTSD relative to CON (Extended Data Fig. 9c), consistent with increased EGR2 expression in CUX2 EXNs (log2FC=0.28, FDR=1.51 × 10 -65 ). Within EXNs, EGR2 was linked to 154 DEGs and to multiple PTSD risk genes, including CAMKV , EGR3 (upregulated), KCNB2 (downregulated), and OPCML (Extended Data Fig. 9d and Supplementary Table 31). Similar to other highly enriched TFs in EXNs (Extended Data Fig. 9e), EGR2 is an immediate early gene. We also detected significant enrichment for motifs of the neuron-associated chromatin remodeler SMARCC1 , which was connected to 168 PTSD snDEGs and 26 PTSD risk variants in EXNs. To test downstream transcriptional effects of these TF networks, we used human induced pluripotent stem cell-derived excitatory neurons and applied lentiviral RNA inter- ference targeting either EGR2 or SMARCC1 , then quantified resulting expression changes (Supplementary Table 39). This perturbation experiment supported changes in 27 DEGs, including CLU and UST (Extended Data Fig. 9d, pink circles). Further, TFAP4 motifs were significantly enriched in INs and OPCs (Extended Data Fig. 9a,f). In INs, TFAP4 was linked to several PTSD GWAS genes, including MAD1L1 , and to 36 DEGs such as ANKRD55 , SPRED2 , and PDE10 (all downregulated), consistent with established transcriptional repressor activity [184] (Extended Data Fig. 9g,h and Supplementary Table 32).

### 6.9 Cell-type-specific cis-regulation at PTSD risk loci

To evaluate how genetic risk is distributed across brain cell types, we performed linkage disequilibrium score regression (LDSC) [213] using GWAS summary statistics for PTSD, PTSD symptom dimensions (hyperarousal, avoidance, and re-experiencing), MDD, additional psychiatric disorders, and a broad set of clinical and non-clinical traits (Fig. 6.5a and Supplementary Table 40). We observed significant enrichment (FDR &lt; 0.05) of PTSD GWAS signal in ATAC peaks from both excitatory neurons (EXNs) and inhibitory neurons (INs), for PTSD (total PCL) as well as for PTSD case-control GWAS. Similar enrichment patterns were also apparent for symptom-based GWAS. In addition, we detected significant enrichment (FDR &lt; 5 × 10 -4 ) for MDD genetic risk within these same neuronal cell types. When compared against bulk DLPFC open chromatin annotations (bCREs; grey dots), the single-nucleus peak sets showed stronger LDSC enrichment across traits (Extended Data Fig. 10a). To further validate these findings, we repeated LDSC using snMultiome to refine risk enrichment at the neuronal subtype level. Relative to the same bCRE background (grey), snMultiome yielded higher LDSC enrichment overall (Fig. 6.5b). Notably, an EXN subtype (OPRK1; -log10P=6.72) was most enriched for the total PCL GWAS, whereas an IN subtype (KCNG1; -log10P=6.15) was most enriched for the PTSD case-control GWAS, suggesting potential subtype specificity that differs between these GWAS phenotypes.

IBD, irritable bowel disease. b, Lollipop plot showing LDSC enrichment of GWAS traits comparing bCREs (grey) versus snMultiome peaks (coloured). The colour of the dot represents the subtype in snMultiome with the highest enrichment value for the trait. c-f, Cis-regulatory architecture at the following GWAS loci and cell types: ELFN1 in IN; and KCNIP4, EGR3 and LRFN5 in EXN. IN and EXN CREs were used to fine-map SNPs for total PCL [77]. For the fine-mapping track, we only plotted SNPs with PIP &gt; 0.0001 for visualization purposes, and only highlight peak-to-gene loops for SNPs with PIP &gt; 0.05, outlined in black if they are within a CRE (lead SNP in red, and MVP SNP in purple). The top two credible SNPs and MVP SNP are labelled. The loops are coloured by cell type (EXN in red and IN in green). Chromosome coordinates are: ELFN1 (chr. 7: 1450000-2350000; c); KCNIP4 (chr. 4: 20600000-22600000; d); EGR3 (chr. 8: 22193302-23193302; e); and LRFN5 (chr. 14: 41105000-4230000; f). NeuN+ TADs are shown above each ideogram, and the coloured triangles correspond to the TAD with significant inter-chromosomal looping in the area.

<!-- image -->

We next fine-mapped PTSD-associated variants within cell-type-specific CREs in EXNs and INs (Methods). Using risk loci reported in two prior GWAS [237, 96], we identified eight loci that overlapped cell-type-specific CREs and prioritized these loci for detailed fine-mapping (Fig. 6.5c-f and Extended Data Fig. 10c-h). We computed posterior inclusion probabilities (PIPs) for candidate PTSD-associated single-nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs) and found that, for 7 of 8 genes, PIP values were strongly correlated with negative log-transformed GWAS P values for SNPs located within CREs (mean correlation=0.769), indicating that fine-mapping captured lead variants with strong association support (Extended Data Fig. 10b). At the MAD1L1 locus, the leading causal variant for total PCL in the Million Veteran Program (MVP) analysis was rs10235664 (PIP=0.01) within IN CREs (Fig. 6.5c). After incorporating IN-specific CRE annotations, we identified additional variants with higher PIPs. In particular, rs62444919 (PIP=0.36) showed strong linkage disequilibrium with rs10235664 (correlation=0.84) and was also predicted to loop to the transcription start site (TSS) of ELFN1 . Peak-to-gene loops to ELFN1 showed high correlation (0.87, FDR=4.29 × 10 -29 ; and 0.67, FDR=2.29 × 10 -13 ), consistent with robust enhancer activity regulating ELFN1 transcription. Using a NeuN+ Hi-C track, we further identified a topologically associated domain (TAD) spanning this region, supporting chromosomal looping between ELFN1 and MAD1L1 . Our approach also recovered a previously reported causal SNP at KCNIP4 , rs4697248 (PIP=0.93), within EXNs (Fig. 6.5d). Finally, we report finemapped risk variants for six additional PTSD genes ( EGR3 , LRFN5 , OPCML , CAMKV , TCF4 , and CRHR1 ) across EXNs and INs together with their corresponding CRE contexts (Fig. 6.5e,f, Extended Data Fig. 10c-h, and Supplementary Table 41). By integrating celltype-specific chromatin annotations into PIP estimation and leveraging TAD structure at these loci, we nominate new putative causal variants and prioritize candidates for follow-up functional genomic validation.

### 6.10 Discussion

Here we present a single-nucleus atlas comprising more than two million nuclei from human prefrontal cortex. To our knowledge, this study represents the largest single-cell genomics analysis of a human brain subregion focused on elucidating the molecular biology of PTSD and MDD. The scale and multi-omic design enabled identification of genes and pathways implicated in PTSD pathophysiology, including glucocorticoid-related processes [47, 98], immune and neuroinflammatory mechanisms [166], and neurotransmission-related programs, particularly GABAergic signalling [99]. By systematically contrasting cases and controls at cell-type resolution, we localized a selective vulnerability of SST interneurons, characterized by a marked reduction in predicted outgoing synaptic signalling to other neuronal populations. We further delineated molecular features that distinguish PTSD from MDD, including microglial inflammatory signalling and neuronal adhesion-related programs (Extended Data Fig. 6). In particular, CCC analyses highlighted increased microglial signalling in MDD through the pro-inflammatory SPP1 pathway, whereas this pathway was reduced in PTSD, consistent with suppression of neuroimmune signalling and microglial activation in PTSD that is not evident in depression.

A striking observation was that endothelial cells exhibited more pronounced transcriptional alterations than any other major cell type, including robust upregulation of FKBP5 , a proposed PTSD risk gene [22] (Fig. 6.1g). Although FKBP5 has often been discussed in the context of neuronal stress responses [223], our results, together with other single-cell transcriptomic studies of the PFC [179], consistently indicate that FKBP5 transcripts are abundant in endothelial cells relative to neurons, pointing to a potentially underappreciated neurovascular component. One possibility is that endothelial FKBP5 changes reflect altered trafficking of peripheral glucocorticoids into the brain parenchyma, and that FKBP5 upregulation represents a compensatory response to the hypocortisolic state frequently reported in PTSD [284, 283]. Under this model, increased glucocorticoid transport across the blood- brain barrier could elevate demands on neurovascular and glial handling of glucocorticoid signalling, which may be accompanied by increased endothelial FKBP5 expression before downstream effects manifest in neurons (Fig. 6.1g).

Beyond transcriptional changes, we identified sets of accessible genomic regions that are likely to contribute to regulatory dysregulation in PTSD. Although candidate CREs can be nominated from ATAC-seq data alone, integration with single-nucleus transcriptomes enables assignment of gene targets by linking open chromatin to expression. Consistent with this framework, we observed substantial overlap between CRE-linked genes and corresponding disease-associated expression changes across cell types (Fig. 6.4e). These analyses allowed us to highlight cell-type-specific cis -regulatory mechanisms that accompany gene expression alterations. In addition, our chromatin enrichment results implicate neurons (both excitatory and inhibitory) as central cellular substrates for genetic risk in PTSD and MDD. By integrating cell-type-resolved accessibility maps with GWAS variant annotations, we resolved candidate cis -regulatory relationships disrupted by credible risk variants at loci including MAD1L1 , KCNIP4 , EGR3 , CRHR1 , TCF4 , and LRFN5 [237, 96]. At the MAD1L1 locus, fine-mapping suggested that putative causal variants within accessible peaks may function as enhancers for the neighboring gene ELFN1 . ELFN1 has been described as an interneuronenriched synaptic protein, and its transcript levels were previously reported to be altered in the PTSD frontal cortex9. Notably, CCC analyses indicated a pronounced reduction in SST IN output, where ELFN1 expression is highest. We observed reduced SST IN communication to most major cell types, with the strongest decreases involving other neuronal populations. Specifically, we detected diminished predicted GABAergic signalling to key receptor targets including GABRA5 , GABBR1 , GABRB1 , and GABRG1 (Fig. 6.3f), and we corroborated these functional consequences in vivo (Fig. 6.3h,k). In parallel, we observed increased glutamatergic signalling through mGluR7, a presynaptic binding partner of ELFN1 that suppresses synaptic release [254]. Elevated mGluR7 signalling is consistent with reduced excitatory drive onto SST INs and a downstream reduction of SST output. Given the tight coupling between ELFN1 and mGluR7 at EXN-IN synapses, we speculate that perturbation of ELFN1-associated regulation could dysregulate mGluR7 signalling and impair circuit-level tuning of excitatory-inhibitory balance. Together, these results support PTSDspecific regulatory effects at this locus that may contribute to SST interneuron vulnerability and reduced GABAergic communication.

This study offers new insight into PTSD and MDD biology, but several limitations should be considered. We assembled a demographically balanced cohort with matching across sex, age, post-mortem interval, and psychiatric diagnosis, and we view inclusion of MDD as a psychiatric control as particularly informative given the high comorbidity between PTSD and depressive disorders [216]. This design allowed us to distinguish convergent from divergent molecular signatures across the two conditions. However, the subgroup of donors with PTSD in the absence of depression remained small (n=8), limiting power to isolate PTSD-specific signals independent of comorbid mood symptoms. In addition, as in many post-mortem brain collections, illegal drug use and drug-related causes of death occurred at moderate rates in both patient cohorts, which may confound disorder-specific molecular effects. Increasing sample sizes and expanding donor stratification will be important in future efforts to better account for these factors.

### 6.11 Methods

#### 6.11.1 Human tissue donors

Human post-mortem brain specimens were obtained from the National Center for PTSD Brain Bank (with consent provided by the next of kin) and from the University of Pittsburgh Tissue Donation Program. Donors included individuals of European and AfricanAmerican ancestry. Male and female donors were group matched by age and post-mortem interval (PMI). Sociodemographic and clinical characteristics are summarized in Extended Data Fig. 1a, including comorbid factors such as tobacco use and other substances of abuse. Inclusion criteria for PTSD, MDD, and control donors required PMI &lt; 48 h and age &gt; 18 and &lt; 85 years. In total, 111 individuals were included (36 PTSD: 19 female and 17 male donors; 36 MDD: 18 female and 18 male donors; and 39 control donors: 18 female and 21 male donors). Tissue was fresh-frozen, and dorsolateral PFC (DLPFC; Brodmann areas 9/46) [175] samples (approximately 25 mg) were collected from each brain.

Psychiatric and demographic information was compiled via post-mortem psychological autopsies, together with review of medical records and toxicology reports. Structured informant interviews were conducted by trained clinicians with individuals knowledgeable about the deceased (typically next of kin). To minimize systematic differences across sources, PTSD, MDD, and control cases from each program were evaluated using the same psychological assessment procedures. DSM-IV consensus diagnoses were assigned by trained clinicians who were not involved in conducting the psychological autopsies. Among the 36 PTSD donors, 75% (27 cases) met criteria for comorbid MDD.

#### 6.11.2 Isolation of nuclei from brain cells for RNA, ATAC and multiome assays

Frozen DLPFC tissue was sectioned on a cryotome into leaflets (approximately 100-300 µ m thick) and stored at -80 ◦ C. Nuclei were isolated using previously described procedures [172, 10, 85], with modifications to ensure compatibility with parallel gene expression and ATAC workflows. To minimize experimental bias, all nuclei isolations were performed by a single operator blinded to sample metadata. Unless otherwise specified, molecular biology grade reagents were obtained from Sigma.

For each sample, 10-20 mg of tissue was placed in 1 ml ice-cold lysis buffer ('buffer A'): 250 mM sucrose, 25 mM KCl, 5 mM MgCl 2 , 10 mM Tricine-KOH (pH 7.8), protease inhibitors without EDTA (Roche), RNase inhibitor (80 U ml -1 ; Roche), 1 mM dithiothreitol,

1% BSA (m/v; Gemini Bio-Products), 0.3% NP-40 (v/v), 0.15 mM spermine, 0.5 mM spermidine, and water. Dithiothreitol, RNase Protector, protease inhibitors, spermine, spermidine, and NP-40 were added immediately before use. The suspension was transferred to a 2-ml Dounce homogenizer (Kimble) and lysed with steady pressure while avoiding air bubbles using pestle A (2 × 5 strokes) followed by pestle B (4 × 5 strokes), performed in five-stroke cycles. Homogenates were filtered through a pre-wetted 40µ m tube-top cell strainer (Corning). All subsequent spins were performed at 4 ◦ C using a refrigerated bench-top centrifuge with a swing-out rotor (Eppendorf). Lysates were centrifuged at 1,000g for 10 min at 4 ◦ C, and pellets were retained and resuspended in 0.4 ml buffer A.

To enrich nuclei, the 0.4 ml resuspension was combined 1:1 with Optiprep solution ('buffer B'): iodixanol 50% (v/v), 25 mM KCl, 5 mM MgCl 2 , 20 mM Tricine-KOH (pH 7.8), protease inhibitors without EDTA, RNase inhibitor (80 U ml -1 ), 1 mM dithiothreitol, and water, yielding a final iodixanol concentration of 25%. The mixture was inverted head-over-tail 10 × . Gradients were prepared by adding 0.6 ml of 40% iodixanol cushion (appropriate mix of buffer A and buffer B) to a 2-ml tube, overlaying with 0.6 ml of 30% iodixanol (appropriate mix of buffer A and buffer B), and finally layering the sample suspension on top. Tubes were centrifuged at 3,000g for 30 min at 4 ◦ C with no brake. After centrifugation, the 30-40% interphase band was collected. From 300 µ l collected, 100 µ l was allocated to gene expression profiling and 200 µ l to ATAC profiling.

For the gene expression fraction, 1,000 µ l of 'RNA wash buffer' was added, and samples were gently mixed and incubated on ice for 10 min. RNA wash buffer contained: RNase Protector (80 U ml -1 ), 1 mM dithiothreitol, 25 mM KCl, 5 mM MgCl 2 , 20 mM Tris-HCl (pH 7.5), 1% (m/v) BSA, 0.1% (v/v) Tween-20, and DPBS (14190, Gibco). For the ATAC fraction, 500 µ l of ATAC lysis buffer was added, mixed thoroughly, and incubated on ice for 5 min. ATAC lysis buffer contained: 10 mM Tris-HCl (pH 7.4), 10 mM NaCl, 3 mM MgCl 2 , 0.01% (v/v) Tween-20, 0.01% (v/v) NP-40, 0.001% (v/v) digitonin, 1% (m/v) BSA, and nuclease-free water. Next, 500 µ l of ATAC wash buffer was added, samples were mixed, and incubated on ice for 5 min. ATAC wash buffer contained: 10 mM Tris-HCl (pH 7.4), 10 mM NaCl, 3 mM MgCl 2 , 0.1% (v/v) Tween-20, 1% (m/v) BSA, and nuclease-free water.

Both RNA and ATAC fractions were centrifuged at 1,000g for 10 min at 4 ◦ C, and supernatants were carefully and completely removed. Nuclei were counted using a haemocytometer. For gene expression, nuclei were resuspended in 'RNA run buffer' (RNase Protector (80 U ml -1 ), 1 mM dithiothreitol, 25 mM KCl, 5 mM MgCl 2 , 20 mM Tris-HCl (pH 7.5), 250 mM sucrose, and water) and adjusted to 1 M nuclei per millilitre. For ATAC, nuclei were resuspended to 3.3 M nuclei per millilitre in ATAC run buffer (1 × Nuclei Buffer (PN2000153, 10X Genomics) and water). Samples were delivered to the Yale Center for Genome Analysis (YCGA) within 15 min and processed according to the 10X Genomics Chromium Single Cell ATAC protocol (CG000168 Rev B, 10X Genomics) and the 10X Genomics Chromium Single Cell 3 ′ Reagent Kits v3 protocol (CG000183 Rev C, 10X Genomics), respectively, targeting 10,000 nuclei per sample.

For assays using 10X Genomics Chromium Next GEM Single Cell Multiome ATAC + Gene Expression (CG000338 Rev A, 10X Genomics), the full 300 µ l post-3,000g fraction was used. The ATAC workflow was followed, with 80 U ml -1 RNase Protector included in ATAC buffers. After the final spin, nuclei were resuspended in 'diluted nuclei buffer' (1 × Nuclei Buffer (PN-2000207), 1 mM dithiothreitol, 1 U ml -1 RNase Protector, and water), counted on a haemocytometer, adjusted to 3.3 M ml -1 , and submitted to YCGA for processing with a target of 10,000 nuclei.

Libraries were sequenced as paired-end 150-bp reads on an Illumina NovaSeq 6000, targeting up to 500 million read pairs per sample for gene expression (RNA) and up to 500 million reads per sample for ATAC.

#### 6.11.3 Xenium in situ transcriptomics

The Xenium In Situ platform quantifies transcript abundance using targeted gene panels. Our assay combined the Xenium Human Brain Panel (n=267 genes) with an additional set of 99 custom-selected genes, primarily curated from DEGs identified in our PTSD singlenucleus atlas, resulting in a total of 366 genes. The full gene list for the Xenium panel is provided in Supplementary Table 23.

Fresh-frozen DLPFC tissue was obtained from the National PTSD Brain Bank for control (n=10), PTSD (n=4), and MDD (n=4) donors. For each participant, frozen blocks were dissected from the superior frontal gyrus within the DLPFC, approximately 1 cm anterior to the genu of the corpus callosum. Tissue blocks were cryosectioned at 10 mm thickness and mounted onto 10X Genomics Xenium slides according to the manufacturer's instructions (10X Genomics protocol CG000580 Rev B). Slides with thaw-mounted tissue were fixed in paraformaldehyde and permeabilized in 70% methanol. After washing, slides were loaded into Xenium slide cassettes and processed through hybridization, ligation, and amplification steps following the Xenium Gene Expression workflow (10X Genomics protocol CG000582 Rev C).

Briefly, the pre-designed Xenium Human Brain Gene Expression panel (266 genes) together with 99 custom probes were applied to tissue sections. Each circularizable DNA probe contained two segments that hybridize to the target RNA and a third segment encoding a gene-specific barcode. Probes were loaded at 10 nM and hybridized overnight at 50 ◦ C. During ligation, probe ends annealed to the target RNA and were ligated to form a circular DNA molecule at 37 ◦ C for 2 h. Circularized probes were then amplified at 30 ◦ C for 2 h, generating multiple copies of the barcode sequence per target transcript.

Processed slides were subsequently imaged on the Xenium Analyzer for in situ detection. Fluorescently labeled oligonucleotides were hybridized to the amplified probe products, and iterative cycles of probe binding, imaging, and probe removal produced barcode-specific optical signatures. These optical patterns were decoded into transcript identities and visualized using Xenium Explorer before downstream bioinformatics analyses.

#### 6.11.4 Animals

All animal husbandry and experimental procedures were conducted in accordance with protocols approved by the Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee at Yale University School of Medicine and the Yale Animal Resources Center. Adult C57BL/6 SST-Cre mice of both sexes (2 months old; n=31 total) were used. Before stress exposure, mice were group housed under standard conditions (23 ◦ C; 12 h:12 h light-dark cycle) with ad libitum access to food and water.

#### 6.11.5 Viral injections

Adult mice were anesthetized with 1-2% isoflurane, secured in a stereotaxic frame, and kept at 37 ◦ C throughout the procedure. Animals received a unilateral injection of AAV-Ef1aDIOhChR2(123T/T159C)-eyfp (500 nl; Addgene) delivered via a Nanoject III (Drummond) at a rate of 50 nl min -1 . After infusion, the glass capillary was left in place for an additional 5-10 min to allow diffusion before withdrawal. Injections targeted the mPFC using the following coordinates: anteroposterior (AP)=+1.5 mm, mediolateral=+0.2 mm, and dorsoventral=+2.4 mm. Mice were allowed to recover for two weeks to permit viral expression before subsequent experiments.

#### 6.11.6 SPS

Mice assigned to the single prolonged stress (SPS) paradigm were handled for 3 consecutive days before stress exposure. On the SPS day, mice (n=16) were subjected to 2 h of restraint in a restrainer, followed immediately by a 20-min forced swim in 23-25 ◦ C water. After a 15- min recovery period, mice were exposed to diethyl ether vapour until loss of consciousness. Animals were then single housed until euthanasia for slice electrophysiology, which was performed 7-14 days after SPS. Control mice (n=15) remained group housed for the duration of the experiment. Following SPS, all mice were weighed daily. Animals were randomly assigned to SPS or control conditions, and behavioural analyses were conducted with the experimenter blinded to group. Behavioural results are provided in Source Data File 1.

#### 6.11.7 Slice electrophysiology

Coronal brain slices containing the mPFC were prepared from control and SPS-exposed male and female mice as previously described [97, 271]. Following decapitation, brains were rapidly extracted and transferred to ice-cold (approximately 4 ◦ C) artificial cerebrospinal fluid (ACSF) in which NaCl was replaced with sucrose (252 mM; sucrose-ACSF) to limit osmotic swelling. Coronal slices (300 µ m) encompassing the mPFC were cut in sucrose-ACSF using an oscillating-blade vibratome (Leica VT1000S). Slices were then moved to the recording chamber mounted on the fixed stage of an Olympus BX50WI microscope for whole-cell patch-clamp recordings. During recordings, slices were continuously perfused with standard ACSF at 2-3 ml min -1 , with temperature maintained at 33 ± 0.5 ◦ C. Standard ACSF (approximately pH 7.35) was equilibrated with 95% O 2 /5% CO 2 and contained: 128 mM NaCl, 3 mM KCl, 2 mM CaCl 2 , 2 mM MgSO 4 , 24 mM NaHCO 3 , 1.25 mM NaH 2 PO 4 , and 10 mM d-glucose.

Pyramidal neurons were identified under infrared differential interference contrast (IR/DIC) optics using a microscope equipped with a × 40 IR objective. Low-resistance patch pipettes (3-5 MΩ) were pulled from patch-clamp glass (Warner Instrument) using a horizontal puller (P-1000, Sutter Instrument). Pipettes were filled with an internal solution containing: 125 mM Cs gluconate, 10 mM HEPES, 5 mM BAPTA-Cs, 10 mM Na 2 -phosphocreatine, 2.38 mM CaCl 2 , 4 mM Mg-ATP, and 0.3 mM Na 2 GTP (pH 7.33). Whole-cell recordings were acquired using an Axoclamp-2B amplifier (Axon Instruments). Signals were low-pass filtered at 3 kHz, amplified 100 × , digitized at 15 kHz, and collected using Clampex 10.5 with Digidata 1550A.

Tonic GABA current was measured in voltage-clamp mode with the membrane potential held at 0 mV. Tonic current was quantified as the difference in holding current measured in the presence of the GABAB receptor agonist baclofen (10 µ M; Tocris Bioscience) versus after subsequent application of the GABAB receptor antagonist CGP 55845 (20 µ M; Tocris Bioscience). Cells were held for 8 min before acquiring a 2-min baseline; recordings were then continued for 5-8 min during each drug condition. For analysis, 1 min of trace at the plateau of each condition was selected. Holding current was estimated using a Gaussian fit to the amplitude histogram of the 1-min segment.

For optogenetic measurements of SST interneuron-driven eIPSCs, pyramidal-cell responses were recorded during delivery of 470-nm blue light through a × 60 objective using a CoolLED pE-800 light source. Light intensity was adjusted to elicit stable single-peak responses (approximately 300 pA at baseline; 0.1-ms stimulation). Ten sweeps were collected per condition and averaged. Average eIPSC peak amplitudes were compared before and after bath application of MRK 016 (1 µ M; MedChemExpress), and the percentage change in IPSC amplitude was calculated. Electrophysiology data are provided in Source Data File 1.

#### 6.11.8 iPS cell-derived glutamatergic neuron transfections

Control human induced pluripotent stem cell (iPS cell)-derived neural progenitor cells (NPCs) from two lines were used: NSB553-S1-1 (male, European ancestry) and NSB2607-1-4 (male, European ancestry; source: National Institute of Mental Health). Both lines were confirmed mycoplasma negative at the time of experimentation. NPCs were maintained on Geltrex (A1413301, Thermo Fisher) in hNPC medium consisting of DMEM/F12 (10565, Life Technologies), 1 × N2 (17502-048, Life Technologies), 1 × B27-RA (12587-010, Life Technologies),

1 × antibiotic-antimycotic, and 20 ng ml -1 FGF2 (Life Technologies). Confluent hiPS cellderived NPCs (1-1.5 × 10 7 cells per well of a six-well plate) were dissociated with Accutase (Innovative Cell Technologies) for 5 min, pelleted (5 min at 1,000g), resuspended, and replated onto Matrigel-coated plates at 3-5 × 10 6 cells per well. Medium was refreshed every 2-3 days for up to 7 days until the next passage.

For neuronal induction, at day -1, confluent hiPS cell-derived NPCs were transduced with lentiviruses encoding rtTA (Addgene 20342) and NGN2 (Addgene 99378). On day 0, NGN2 expression was induced by adding doxycycline (dox; 1 µ g ml -1 ). On day 1, transduced cultures were subjected to antibiotic selection for lentiviral integration using G-418 (1 mg ml -1 ). On day 3, cultures were transitioned from NPC medium to neuronal medium composed of BrainPhys (05790, Stemcell Technologies), 1 × N2 (17502-048, Life Technologies), 1 × B27RA (12587-010, Life Technologies), natural mouse laminin (1 µ g ml -1 ; Life Technologies), BDNF (20 ng ml -1 ; 450-02, Peprotech), GDNF (20 ng ml -1 ; 450-10, Peprotech), dibutyryl cyclic-AMP (500 µ g ml -1 ; D0627, Sigma), and l-ascorbic acid (200 nM; A0278, Sigma), with dox maintained at 1 µ g ml -1 . Thereafter, 50% of the medium was replaced with fresh neuronal medium every other day.

On day 5, immature NGN2-induced neurons were replated onto Geltrex-coated plates. Cells were dissociated with Accutase for 5-10 min, washed with DMEM, gently resuspended, counted, and centrifuged at 1,000g for 5 min. Pellets were resuspended in neuronal medium and seeded at 1.2 × 10 6 cells per well in 12-well plates. To limit proliferation of non-neuronal cells, cultures were treated on day 11 with Ara-C (200 nM; C6645, Sigma), followed by half-medium changes. On day 17, Ara-C (cytosine β -d-arabinofuranoside hydrochloride) was fully removed via a complete medium change, and cultures were infected with pooled short hairpin RNA (shRNA) lentiviral vectors targeting transcription factors (Sigma-Aldrich). For SMARCC1 , shRNAs TRCN0000015632, TRCN0000015631, TRCN0000015630, and TRCN0000015628 were used; for EGR2 , shRNAs TRCN0000013841, TRCN0000013840, TRCN0000013839, and TRCN0000013838 were used. The total multiplicity of infection was 1.0. An MOI-matched scrambled shRNA control (SHC016; SigmaAldrich) was included. Medium was replaced with non-viral medium 4 h after infection.

On day 19, transduced neurons were selected using puromycin (1 µ g ml -1 ) with continued half-medium changes until harvest. Neurons were collected in TRIzol (Invitrogen) on day 21 and processed for bulk RNA sequencing at the Yale Center for Genome Analysis. RNA integrity number and concentration were assessed using a Bioanalyzer (Agilent). Libraries were prepared with the SMARTer Stranded RNA-seq Kit (Takara Bio) following rRNA depletion from 1 µ g total RNA. Samples were barcoded, pooled (eight per lane), and sequenced as 75-bp paired-end reads on an Illumina HiSeq4000 to a depth of 50 million reads per sample.

#### 6.11.9 snRNA-seq processing and analysis pipeline

Gene-by-cell count matrices were generated by aligning sequencing reads to the hg38 reference genome using 10X Genomics CellRanger [298] (v6.1.1). To reduce ambient RNA contamination and mitigate sample-specific technical artefacts, we applied CellBender [83] (v0.2.2) using the remove-background workflow, which outputs an HDF5 file after filtering empty droplets and estimating background contributions. Putative doublets were then identified and removed from each sample using two complementary methods, Scrublet [272] (v0.2.3) and DoubletDetection [276] (v4.2).

For sample-level aggregation, we processed and clustered each sample using Pegasus [154] (v1.5.0), a Python framework for single-cell transcriptomic analysis. We manually inspected UMAPembeddings for all samples and excluded six low-quality samples characterized by low cell-type heterogeneity, yielding a final set of 105 snRNA-seq samples. Samples were then combined into a single object and filtered at the cell level using the following thresholds: mitochondrial fraction ≤ 10%, at least 200 detected genes, and at least 500 unique molecular identifiers. Mitochondrial, sex, and ribosomal genes were excluded, and only robustly expressed genes were retained for downstream analyses. The resulting snRNA-seq dataset comprised 935,371 cells and 27,982 genes.

For clustering and annotation, the filtered matrix was log-normalized. We selected the top 2,000 highly variable genes, performed principal component analysis, and retained the top 50 components. Batch effects were corrected using Harmony. The batch-corrected 50-dimensional embedding was used to construct a k-nearest-neighbours graph (k=100), followed by Leiden clustering to define cell clusters. After an initial clustering pass, markergene profiles were used to identify and remove clusters with mixed identities or inconsistent marker expression, and clustering was repeated on the refined dataset.

Cell-type annotation followed marker sets from Ma et al.18 using three hierarchical resolutions: (1) seven canonical cell types, (2) 14 major neuronal and non-neuronal subtypes, and (3) 61 transcriptomic subtypes. The seven canonical classes were EXN, IN, OLG, OPC, END, AST, and MG. The 14 subtypes were CUX2, RORB, FEZF2, OPRK1, LAMP5, KCNG1, VIP, SST, PVALB, OLG, OPC, END, AST, and MG. Finally, we subclustered each of the 14 subtypes and assigned subtype labels using Ma et al.18 markers, producing 61 distinct transcriptomic subtypes at the finest resolution (Extended Data Fig. 2d,e).

#### 6.11.10 snATAC-seq processing and analysis pipeline

Peak-by-cell count matrices were generated by aligning reads to the hg38 reference genome using 10X Genomics CellRanger ATAC [219] (v2.0.0). Using CellRanger outputs, we performed quality control and filtering in ArchR [103] (v1.0.2) by constructing Arrow files for each sample and retaining cells that passed filterTSS =4 and filterFrags =1000. ArchR quality control leverages multiple metrics, including (1) the number of unique nuclear fragments, (2) signal-to-background enrichment, and (3) fragment-length distributions. Consistent with nucleosomal periodicity, we expected depletion of fragments corresponding to nucleosome-wrapped DNA (approximately 147 bp). To identify potential multiplets, we com- puted doublet scores using addDoubletScores , and subsequently removed inferred doublets using filterDoublets before downstream analyses.

For sample aggregation, we manually reviewed quality-control summaries and excluded lowquality datasets, including ATAC counterparts of the six excluded snRNA-seq samples described above, as well as samples lacking a matched RNA profile. This filtering yielded 94 snATAC-seq samples, which were combined into a single ArchR project used for all subsequent analyses. For dimensionality reduction and clustering, we applied iterative latent semantic indexing (LSI) via addIterativeLSI . Clusters were then identified in the reduced space using addClusters (Seurat graph-based clustering by default). Low-dimensional structure was visualized using UMAP ( addUMAP and plotEmbedding ). Cluster annotations were assigned using marker loci from Lake et al. [150]. After marker-based inspection, we manually refined the UMAP and repeated dimensionality reduction and clustering twice, resulting in a final embedding of 473,321 cells annotated into seven canonical cell types: EXN, IN, OLG, OPC, END, AST, and MG.

#### 6.11.11 snMultiome processing and analysis pipeline

Raw FASTQ files were processed with CellRanger ARC (v2.0.2) using hg38 as the reference genome to generate initial RNA and ATAC count matrices. For per-sample preprocessing, quality control, and filtering, we used Signac [241] (v1.11.0), an R framework that integrates single-cell chromatin accessibility analyses with Seurat and supports joint handling of RNA and ATAC assays within a single object. Each snMultiome dataset was loaded as a Seurat object containing paired RNA and ATAC modalities. Cells were retained if they met the following criteria: nCount ATAC &gt; 1,000, nCount RNA &gt; 200, and TSS.enrichment &gt; 2. Putative doublets in the RNA modality were identified and removed using DoubletDetection.

For aggregation, we clustered each sample independently in Signac and manually reviewed UMAP embeddings to exclude low-quality datasets. A total of 25 snMultiome samples were retained and merged into a single integrated object. For clustering and annotation, gene expression was normalized using SCTransform, followed by dimensionality reduction with principal component analysis. For chromatin accessibility, we performed latent semantic indexing to address sparsity using FindTopFeatures , RunTFIDF , and RunSVD . We then constructed a joint neighbour graph that integrates both modalities using Seurat v4 weighted nearest-neighbour modeling via FindMultiModalNeighbors . The joint embedding was visualized with RunUMAP , and the resulting 119,431 cells were annotated into seven canonical cell types and 14 subtypes (matching the snRNA-seq definitions) using canonical marker genes. For peak calling, the integrated Seurat object was converted into an ArchR project, and peaks were called separately for the seven cell types and the 14 subtypes.

#### 6.11.12 Xenium processing and analysis pipeline

For segmentation, we processed Xenium data using both cell-based and nucleus-based boundaries. Cell boundaries were obtained using the default Xenium Analyzer segmentation, which expands outward from DAPI-stained nuclei by 15 µ m. To generate nucleus-restricted segmentations, we resegmented data with Xenium Ranger (v2.0) using the resegment command with --expansion-distance=0 and --resegment-nuclei=true .

For quality control and filtering, we used Squidpy33 (v1.6.1) to compute per-sample UMAP embeddings and examine standard metrics, including total and unique transcript counts per cell or nucleus. After manual review of the UMAP structure and quality-control summaries for each sample, we retained and aggregated 18 Xenium datasets for downstream analyses.

Clustering and annotation were performed independently for the soma-segmented dataset (scXenium) and the nucleus-segmented dataset (snXenium). This yielded 550,520 cells in the scXenium embedding and 523,314 nuclei in the snXenium embedding, with both representations restricted to the same fixed 366-gene panel. Clusters were annotated into the seven canonical brain cell types, and DEG analyses were subsequently performed at this cell- type resolution. Differential expression was computed using MAST for both scXenium and snXenium, and resulting signatures were compared with the snRNA-seq MAST results. For Xenium, we used thresholds | FC | &gt; 1 . 1 and FDR &lt; 0.01 (scXenium and snXenium), whereas snRNA-seq used | FC | &gt; 1 . 2 and FDR &lt; 0.01.

For visualization, we used SpatialData [177] (v0.1.2) to render snXenium slides, enabling display of cell-type-annotated nuclei and individual transcripts at their native spatial coordinates. To illustrate spatial differences for selected genes across the DLPFC, we show representative slides from one CON donor and one PTSD donor; however, statistical comparisons of expression were computed using all CON samples versus all PTSD samples. For each highlighted gene, we additionally report a bar plot of log-normalized counts comparing CON versus PTSD expression across the seven canonical cell types.

#### 6.11.13 Differential abundance analysis

We tested for condition-associated shifts in cell-type composition within each modality using single-cell compositional data analysis with scCODA (v0.1.9) [36]. For snRNA-seq and snMultiome, differential abundance was evaluated across the 14 annotated subtypes, whereas for snATAC-seq and Xenium we performed the analysis at the level of the seven canonical cell types. Within each modality, we applied compositional analysis with reference cell type= 'automatic' , which selects as the reference the cell type exhibiting the smallest proportional change across conditions. We ran comparisons for PTSD versus CON, MDD versus CON, and PTSD versus MDD to assess compositional differences among the three diagnostic groups. Using an FDR threshold of 0.05, we detected a significant reduction in oligodendrocytes (OLGs) in PTSD relative to both CON and MDD.

#### 6.11.14 Differential gene expression analysis

Differential expression in snRNA-seq data was evaluated for each of the 14 subtypes using four complementary methods: (1) MAST19 [82] (v1.21.3) with covariate adjustment for age at death, sex, ancestry, PMI, RNA integrity number, and library size; (2) a Wilcoxon rank-sum test without covariate correction implemented via FindMarkers in Seurat [106] (v5.2.1); (3) nebula [109] (v1.5.3), a mixed-model framework used to account for samplelevel effects, with covariate correction; and (4) a sample-level pseudobulk analysis using DESeq2 [167] (v1.46.0) with covariate correction. For MAST and Wilcoxon, raw counts were CPM (counts per million) normalized and log-transformed before testing. We restricted testing to robustly expressed genes, defined as genes detected in at least 5% of cells in either condition. For nebula, we required expression in at least 20% of cells in either condition and excluded samples with fewer than 30 cells to enable stable dispersion estimation. In nebula , we used an offset term combining per-cell UMI and gene counts (0.8 × nCount + 0.2 × nFeature), a negative binomial gamma mixed model, kappa =800, and cutoff cell =20. For DESeq2, genes with fewer than 20 reads per sample or CPM &lt; 0.5 were removed, and MDD samples with exceptionally low read depth were excluded. We then applied settings commonly used for single-cell pseudobulk workflows, including test='LRT' , minmu =1 × 10 -6 , and minReplicatesForReplace =Inf. Significance thresholds were set to | FC | &gt; 1 . 2 and FDR &lt; 0.01 for MAST and Wilcoxon, FDR &lt; 0.1 for nebula, and FDR &lt; 0.05 for DESeq2 pseudobulk.

For snRNA-seq, we first contrasted PTSD versus CON and MDD versus CON using all available samples within each diagnostic group. We defined PTSD snDEGs (n=1,184) and MDD snDEGs (n=1,918) as the intersection of MAST and Wilcoxon DEGs with concordant directionality across the seven canonical cell types. These snRNA-seq DEG sets were compared with bulk RNA-seq DEG signatures reported by Girgenti et al.9 and Chatzinakos et al.26. The resulting DEG lists were used in downstream analyses, including cross-disorder comparisons between PTSD and MDD, overlap analyses with CRE-linked genes (CLGs) from snATAC-seq, and identification of putative downstream targets of cell-type-specific transcription factors.

We also assessed sex-associated differences by first performing a baseline comparison of CON (male) versus CON (female), followed by condition-stratified contrasts: PTSD (male) versus CON (male), PTSD (female) versus CON (female), MDD (male) versus CON (male), and MDD(female) versus CON (female). To examine the influence of comorbid depression within PTSD, we performed PTSD (+MDD) versus CON and PTSD ( -MDD) versus CON analyses and compared these results with the full PTSD versus CON signature using a three-way set relationship analysis. For snXenium and scXenium, we computed DEGs using MAST and compared effect patterns to snRNA-seq MAST results to provide spatial validation of DEG signals.

#### 6.11.15 PTSD versus MDD cross-disorder comparison

To assess shared versus disorder-specific transcriptional signatures, we first intersected the PTSD snDEGs and MDD snDEGs, identifying 502 genes unique to PTSD and 1,236 genes unique to MDD. We then applied Rank-Rank Hypergeometric Overlap [37] (v1.46.0) to quantify concordance and discordance between PTSD and MDD in a threshold-free framework. This analysis identified 12 genes that were significantly discordant across cell types (downregulated in PTSD and upregulated in MDD).

To further probe PTSD-specific effects independent of comorbid depression, we stratified the PTSD cohort into donors with co-occurring MDD (n=27) and those without MDD (n=8), and performed DEG analyses for each subgroup versus CON to nominate genes enriched in PTSD without depression. In addition, we derived PTSD-specific and MDD-specific DEG sets from the snXenium and scXenium MAST analyses for spatial cross-validation of disorder-associated transcriptional changes.

#### 6.11.16 Gene Ontology enrichment analysis

Gene Ontology (GO) enrichment was performed using the Enrichr R package [48] (v3.4). In total, we ran five Enrichr-based GO analyses using the following input gene sets: PTSD snDEGs, PTSD cell-type-specific DEGs, the 502 PTSD-specific DEGs, PTSD nebula DEGs, and PTSD DESeq2 pseudobulk DEGs. For each analysis, we queried both the Gene Ontology Molecular Function 2023 and Gene Ontology Biological Process 2023 libraries via the Enrichr function. For visualization, we retained the highest-ranking FDR-significant terms and removed redundant terms when generating GO bar plots.

#### 6.11.17 CCC analysis

We inferred cell-cell communication (CCC) using the standard CellChat workflow [127] (v1.5.0), which leverages single-cell expression of curated ligands and receptors to reconstruct intercellular signaling networks. Analyses were performed using the normalized snRNAseq count matrix together with the corresponding cell-type annotations. For each possible 'sender' (ligand-expressing) and 'receiver' (receptor-expressing) cell-type pair, CellChat estimates a communication strength that increases when ligands are highly expressed in the sender population and their cognate receptors are highly expressed in the receiver population (and decreases under the converse).

The number of inferred ligand-receptor interactions can vary with the approach used to summarize gene expression within each cell group. We therefore used CellChat's robust mean estimator, truncatedMean , with the default trimming parameter trim =0.05. CCC inference was performed separately for the PTSD and CON groups, and we then compared the resulting networks to identify condition-associated differences. To mitigate global differences attributable to batch effects or other technical factors, we normalized the 3D communication tensors (sender cell types × receiver cell types × ligand-receptor interactions) from each CellChat object to the same total sum. We then collapsed each tensor across ligand-receptor interactions to obtain a 2D CCC network and constructed a differential communication network by subtracting the CON network from the PTSD network. For visualization, node colors reflected aggregated outgoing communication strength (sending) or aggregated incoming communication volume (receiving), corresponding to output and input, respectively.

To emphasize specific communication changes, we additionally extracted relevant slices of the 3D tensor (that is, restricting to microglia (MG) as the sender and considering all receivers across ligand-receptor interactions), thereby reducing the tensor to a 2D representation for targeted interpretation.

#### 6.11.18 Neuronal-specific CCC analysis

Neuronal-focused cell-cell communication was inferred using NeuronChat [297] (v1.1.0), which is tailored to neural ligand-receptor signaling and neurotransmitter-related communication. We used the normalized snRNA-seq expression matrix together with cell-type annotations as input. Because inferred interactions depend on how expression is summarized within each cell group, we used NeuronChat's default robust averaging approach, trimean , with trim =0.1. This estimator places greater weight on the second quartile of expression and lower weight on the third and fourth quartiles, thereby reducing sensitivity to extreme values.

NeuronChat analyses were run separately for the PTSD and CON groups, followed by comparative differential analyses. For cross-condition comparisons, we normalized the 3D communication tensors (sender cell types × receiver cell types × neuroligand-receptor interactions) to have the same total sum in each NeuronChat object to reduce the influence of batch effects across snRNA-seq samples. We then collapsed each tensor across neuroligandreceptor interactions to obtain 2D CCC networks, and constructed a differential network by subtracting the CON network from the PTSD network. To summarize sender activity, we aggregated ligand-based communication strengths to compute total outgoing signal- ing for each cell type, and quantified changes in sending output as the difference between PTSD and CON. Finally, we examined pathway-specific contributions by extracting selected neurotransmitter-receptor interaction subsets (that is, slices of the 3D tensors) to determine which neuroligand-receptor pathways accounted for the observed PTSD-CON differences.

#### 6.11.19 Peak calling on snATAC-seq clusters

To mitigate sparsity inherent to single-nucleus ATAC-seq, we generated pseudobulk replicates for each of the seven canonical cell types using addGroupCoverages in ArchR. Peaks were then called on each pseudobulk replicate with MACS2 [296] (v2.2.9.1). ArchR constructs reproducible peak sets via an iterative overlap-merging strategy implemented in addReproduciblePeakSet , producing fixed-width peak intervals. Briefly, peaks are ranked by statistical significance; the most significant peak is retained and all peaks overlapping it are removed from consideration; this procedure is iterated over the remaining peaks until no candidates remain. ArchR applies this iterative overlap removal across all pseudobulk replicates for a given cell type, then evaluates peak reproducibility across replicates and retains only peaks meeting the threshold specified by the reproducibility parameter, yielding a single merged peak set per cell type.

To obtain a unified reference peak set, we next merged cell-type-specific peak sets by renormalizing peak significance across cell types and repeating the iterative overlap-removal procedure. This produced a single union peak set consisting of fixed-width (501 bp) peaks. Depending on the downstream analysis, we used either the cell-type-specific peak sets or the union peak set. We added a peak-by-cell matrix based on union peaks using addPeakMatrix , which served as input for downstream analyses including peak-to-gene linkage and transcription factor motif inference.

To benchmark peak-calling quality, we intersected our cell-type-specific snATAC-seq peaks with adult bulk brain bCREs [207]. Adult bCREs were derived from 96 DNase-seq experi- ments and assembled from ENCODE V4 cCREs [250]. Specifically, we selected V4 cCREs with Z-score &gt; 1.64, removed elements with limited experimental support, and retained only those supported by at least five experiments, yielding 253,638 adult bCREs. Overlap between snATAC-seq peak sets and adult bCREs was computed with bedtools intersect (v2.30.0), using a minimum overlap fraction ( -f ) ranging from 0.2 to 1.0 to quantify the percent overlap.

#### 6.11.20 snRNA-seq and snATAC-seq integration

We integrated snATAC-seq profiles with a subset of snRNA-seq data comprising six highquality samples with substantial cell-type heterogeneity using ArchR's addGeneIntegrationMatrix . We adopted a constrained integration strategy, in which snRNA-seq cells are restricted to match their best-aligned counterparts in snATAC-seq within the seven canonical cell types. Using the resulting GeneIntegrationMatrix , we inferred peak-to-gene associations with addPeak2GeneLinks , which uses the integrated RNA modality to compute correlations between chromatin accessibility at peaks and expression of nearby genes. Long-range associations were allowed by setting maxDist =2 MB. For visualization of linked regulatory and gene regions, we used plotPeak2GeneHeatmap with k-means clustering set to 20 to generate paired heatmaps of ATAC peaks and their associated gene targets.

#### 6.11.21 Characterization of cell-type-specific CREs

After constructing peak-to-gene linkages, we nominated candidate regulatory elements by retaining peak-gene pairs with correlation &gt; 0.4 and FDR &lt; 0.05, corresponding to peaks most likely to influence transcription. Using this criterion, we identified 395,932 cell-typespecific CREs (defined as peaks unique to a given cell type within this set), which served as the foundation for downstream ATAC-based analyses. For each cell type, we derived CRE-linked genes (CLGs) from the retained peak-to-gene links and intersected CLGs with cell-type-specific DEGs to define CLG-DEG sets. These cell-type-specific CREs were also used in GWAS-focused analyses, including LDSC-based trait heritability enrichment and cell-type-resolved fine-mapping of putative causal variants at PTSD risk loci.

#### 6.11.22 Characterization of condition-specific CREs

To examine epigenomic differences between PTSD and MDD, we assembled condition-specific CRE sets from the ATAC data. Peaks were called separately within each cell-type-bycondition group (for example, EXN CON, EXN MDD, and EXN PTSD) using the same peakcalling procedure applied to the seven canonical cell types. Condition-unique peaks were then defined using bedtools intersect . After adding peak-to-gene links and applying the same CRE definition (correlation &gt; 0.4, FDR &lt; 0.05), we identified 141,939 condition-specific CREs across all cell-type-by-condition groups. For each cell type, PTSD CLG-DEGs were defined by intersecting PTSD-specific CLGs with PTSD DEGs, and MDD CLG-DEGs were defined analogously by intersecting MDD-specific CLGs with MDD DEGs. At the global level, this yielded 383 PTSD-specific CLG-DEGs and 1,063 MDD-specific CLG-DEGs that were unique to each disorder.

#### 6.11.23 snMultiome ATAC analysis

Because snMultiome provided neuronal subtype resolution, we performed peak calling at both the seven canonical cell-type level and across the 14 annotated subtypes. We assessed peak-set quality by comparing snMultiome-derived peaks to bulk brain bCREs and by evaluating concordance with the cell-type-specific snATAC-seq peak sets. Overlap between snATAC-seq and snMultiome peak sets was quantified using bedtools intersect with a minimum overlap of one nucleotide. We computed an overlap ratio by dividing the number of intersecting peaks by the total number of snATAC-seq peaks. Finally, leveraging subtype-resolved neuronal peak annotations, we used LDSC to identify the excitatory or inhibitory neuronal subtypes showing the strongest enrichment for specific traits.

#### 6.11.24 Transcription factor motif enrichment analysis and footprinting

We quantified transcription factor motif enrichment to infer regulatory factors with cell-typespecific activity. In ArchR, addMotifAnnotations was used to annotate motif occurrences within the union peak set, encoding motif presence (P &lt; 5 × 10 -5 ) as a binary motif-by-peak matrix. Motif enrichment in either cell-type marker peaks or differential peak sets was then tested using peakAnnoEnrichment . In addition to these enrichment analyses, we used chromVAR to estimate TF-associated activity at single-cell resolution while accounting for technical confounders. Specifically, addDeviationsMatrix was used to compute per-cell deviation z-scores for each motif by comparing the observed number of fragments overlapping motif-containing peaks against an expected count derived from a matched background set that controls for biases including GC content, PCR amplification, and Tn5 tagmentation.

For TF footprinting, we obtained motif positions with getPositions , constructed pseudobulk coverages per cell type with addGroupCoverages , and computed footprints using getFootprints . In plotFootprints , ArchR normalizes the footprint signal by estimating and subtracting Tn5 sequence bias. We visualized footprint profiles for EGR2 and TFAP4 , contrasting PTSD versus CON in EXNs and INs, respectively.

#### 6.11.25 Transcription factor gene-regulatory network construction

For each cell type, we assembled transcription factor-CRE-gene linkages and annotated target genes with DEG status (up/down) and whether the gene was implicated by PTSD GWAS (true/false). We then constructed gene-regulatory networks (GRNs) for EXNs and INs using TF-CRE-gene links derived from three highly enriched transcription factors per cell type. Networks were visualized using igraph (v1.2.6), depicting TF-to-target relationships while overlaying gene attributes such as DEG status and PTSD GWAS membership. For visualization, we applied correlation thresholds of 0.7 for EXNs and 0.6 for INs. In the EXN GRN, we additionally highlighted in pink target genes that overlapped with DEGs observed in the iPS cell perturbation experiments targeting EGR2 and SMARCC1 . Across all TF-CRE-gene analyses, statistical significance was assessed using the FDR associated with TF-CRE linkages to account for multiple comparisons.

#### 6.11.26 Estimating GWAS enrichment using ATAC and Multiome peaks

We used linkage disequilibrium score regression (LDSC) [35] (v1.0.1) to quantify heritability enrichment of complex traits within cell-type-resolved open chromatin. GWAS summary statistics were collected for PTSD-related phenotypes (total PCL, case-control, reexperiencing, avoidance, and hyperarousal), additional psychiatric traits (schizophrenia, MVP depression, Alzheimer's disease, and alcoholism), and non-psychiatric traits (irritable bowel disease, diabetes, height, and education). Summary statistics were formatted using munge sumstats.py and lifted to hg38 coordinates with UCSC liftOver . Cell-type-specific peak sets were converted to LDSC annotations using make annotation.py , and LD scores were computed for each annotation with ldsc.py . Enrichment P values were adjusted for multiple testing using Benjamini-Hochberg correction. We compared LDSC enrichments obtained from single-cell peak sets (snATAC-seq or snMultiome) against bulk bCRE annotations and observed stronger trait enrichments when using single-cell-derived regulatory maps.

#### 6.11.27 GWAS fine-mapping

To screen PTSD GWAS loci, we first compiled risk regions reported in two prior PTSD GWAS [237, 96]. For each locus, we defined an approximately 1-2 Mb window centered to include the MVP lead SNP. Using GWAS summary statistics for total PCL and reexperiencing, we fine-mapped all candidate regions and then prioritized eight loci whose variants overlapped cell-type-specific CREs in EXNs and/or INs.

To incorporate epigenomic information as prior evidence, we performed fine-mapping with the sum of single effects model (SuSiE; v0.12.35) [264]. Specifically, for SNPs located within snATAC-seq peaks, we used peak enrichment scores ( -log 10 P ), which reflect peak strength, as prior weights to preferentially prioritize variants supported by strong ATAC signal [1]. Across the union peak set, enrichment scores ranged from 1 to 507 (mean 19.7; s.d. 28.3). To limit the influence of extreme outliers, scores above the 95th percentile were capped at the 95th-percentile value (67.49) and then rescaled to the range 1-100. Variants outside ATAC peaks were assigned a small uniform prior weight of 0.1. We restricted fine-mapping to CREs defined by peak-to-gene linkage (correlation &gt; 0.4 and FDR &lt; 0.05).

Fine-mapping was carried out using SuSiE regression with susie rss and PTSD GWAS summary statistics (total PCL and re-experiencing) [237]. SuSiE outputs posterior inclusion probabilities (PIPs), which represent the probability that a given SNP is causally associated with the trait. Linkage disequilibrium (LD) was estimated using 503 European-ancestry samples from the 1000 Genomes Project (1KG). We used the 1KG Phase 3 reference panel [1], obtained via the Resources section of the PLINK 2.0 website (Resources - PLINK 2.0 (coggenomics.org)). European samples were selected based on 1KG super-population labels, and duplicate and ambiguous SNPs were removed. Quality control was performed with PLINK [209], retaining SNPs with a 99% genotyping rate and minor allele frequency ≥ 0.005, excluding samples with &gt; 5% missing genotypes, and removing markers failing Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium tests. The resulting reference set comprised 503 non-overlapping European samples genotyped at 8,190,311 SNPs.

Genomic coordinates for risk variants were obtained using the UCSC Genome Browser [135] with the GRCh38/hg38 assembly. For fine-mapping visualizations, we integrated multiple tracks within the predefined locus windows, including ATAC signal, CRE-gene links connecting to the transcription start site (TSS) of the nominated risk gene, SuSiE PIP values, LD (correlation) relative to the lead SNP, and gene annotations. We plotted all variants with PIP &gt; 0.0001, highlighted CRE-gene loops for variants with PIP &gt; 0.05, and outlined variants in black when they fell within a CRE. The top two credible SNPs and the MVP lead SNP were labelled, with the lead SNP colored red and the MVP SNP colored purple. CRE-gene loops were colored by their CRE-gene correlation values and by cell type (IN in green; EXN in red).

To annotate higher-order chromatin structure, we identified topologically associated domains (TADs) for ELFN1 , KCNIP4 , EGR3 , and LRFN5 using a NeuN+ Hi-C dataset [208]. Contact domains were called with the Juicer [78] (v1.6) arrowhead command using parameters r = 10 , 000 (matching the Hi-C resolution) and m = 1 , 000 (sliding-window size along the diagonal). These TAD annotations were used to contextualize chromosomal looping patterns in the fine-mapping figures.

### Chapter 7

## Conclusion and Outlook

This chapter summarizes the key contributions and findings in the dissertation. It then continue to note the limitations of the current research and highlight the future works to strengthen the research direction.

### 7.1 Summary of Contributions and Key Findings

This dissertation set out to address a practical and scientific gap in single-cell genomics: high-throughput assays now measure cellular heterogeneity at unprecedented scale, yet the resulting data are fragmented across modalities, limited by assay-specific noise and sparsity, and confounded by differences in cohort composition and experimental protocols. These constraints make it difficult to connect observations to mechanistic regulatory programs, and even harder to translate molecular signals into interpretable hypotheses that can be tested in downstream experiments or linked to disease biology. The central thesis of this work is that interpretable AI can turn incomplete multi-omic measurements into actionable regulatory reasoning, by learning robust representations, translating across modalities under realistic missingness, and producing explanations that function as in-silico perturbations rather than purely post-hoc descriptions.

Across the chapters, the contributions follow a consistent arc from modality translation to regulatory reasoning. Early chapters develop assay-aware representation learning and transfer strategies that leverage high-quality references to stabilize learning under sparse epigenomic readouts. Building on that foundation, the dissertation introduces cross-modality learning in settings where paired measurements are unavailable, establishing principled ways to align modalities and translate between them while maintaining biological structure. These translation models are then used not as an endpoint, but as a bridge toward mechanistic inquiry: by enabling controlled in-silico queries the models support hypotheses about regulatory control at the level of cell states and lineages.

A key methodological step in moving beyond translation is to represent regulation as combinatorial and redundant. Gene expression is often governed not by a single locus or feature, but by sets of cis-regulatory elements that exhibit partial overlap in function across contexts. The dissertation advances a framework for identifying such sets through learnable subset perturbations, providing an interpretable interface that summarizes not only 'important' signals but also minimal or alternative regulatory explanations consistent with the learned model. This reframes interpretability as a form of structured reasoning: compact, testable regulatory mechanisms.

Finally, the dissertation demonstrates these ideas in consortium-scale brain and disease settings, where integration across large cohorts and multiple modalities becomes essential. In these environments, the work emphasizes the full pipeline: harmonizing heterogeneous data, learning transferable representations, validating signals with orthogonal evidence, and using interpretable AI to prioritize regulatory programs and disease-relevant mechanisms at the level of specific cell types and states. Taken together, the dissertation contributes (i) algorithms for robust learning under assay limitations, (ii) translation frameworks that operate under partial pairing and missingness, (iii) interpretability methods that support combina- torial regulatory reasoning, and (iv) an applied blueprint for deploying these ideas in real neurobiological studies where scale, confounding, and validation constraints are unavoidable.

### 7.2 Limitations

While this dissertation advances interpretable AI for single-cell multi-omic systems, its conclusions are bounded by the modalities and study designs that are currently most available at scale. Much of the methodological development is RNA- and chromatin-accessibility-centric, and translation or integration across partially observed modalities necessarily compresses biological signals that may be uniquely represented in protein abundance, methylation, 3D genome architecture, or spatial microenvironmental context. In addition, many of the most consequential applications occur under distribution shift-across cohorts, brain regions, protocols, ancestries, and disease contexts-where batch effects, peak-set mismatch, and compositional differences can create failure modes that appear biologically plausible unless explicitly modeled.

A second boundary is interpretability and causality. Interpretable outputs (e.g. attribution, perturbation, subset selection) can be sensitive to model misspecification and dataset shift, so they require uncertainty estimates, stability checks, and corroboration with orthogonal evidence rather than being treated as definitive mechanisms. More fundamentally, much of the available brain data is observational and often cross-sectional, which limits causal claims about variant-driven regulation and downstream phenotypes. These constraints motivate the next phase of work: expanding modality coverage while treating missingness and domain shift as first-class problems, adopting foundation-model backbones that are both transferable and uncertainty-aware, and building disease-focused pipelines that translate regulatory hypotheses into readable, testable genotype-to-phenotype graphs.

### 7.3 Future Directions

A near-term direction is broader and more principled modality integration while treating missingness as a primary design constraint rather than an inconvenience. This suggests a shift from point imputation to uncertainty-aware generative integration, where models learn modality-consistent distributions, detect domain mismatch, and explicitly communicate confidence. Foundation-model approaches can unify these goals by learning transferable cellular and regulatory representations across studies and modalities, providing a stable backbone for translation, alignment, and perturbation-style reasoning at consortium scale.

A second direction is to connect computational outputs to human interpretation and action. LLMs can serve as a grounded interface that turns complex model outputs into readable, evidence-linked hypotheses-standardizing 'mechanism summaries' that specify implicated cell types, regulatory elements, target genes, directionality, and uncertainty without introducing unsupported claims. Building on this, the most impactful applications will be disease-focused: for psychiatric and neurodegenerative disorders, the goal is a reproducible model that links genotype to regulatory elements, regulatory programs, cell-state changes, tissue- and circuit-level phenotypes, and ultimately clinical symptoms. Such models should prioritize credible causal variants and mechanistic pathways that remain robust and testable across cohorts.

#### Appendix C

## Supplementary Information for Chapter 4

### C.1 Supplementary Methods

#### C.1.1 Model details

###### scATAC-seq autoencoder details

In our model architecture, we implemented a more intricate design for the autoencoder dedicated to the ATAC modality, denoted by f R Enc for the encoder and f R Dec for the decoder. To create an effective representation of the open chromatin peaks, we initiated the process by grouping these peaks according to their respective chromosomes, thereby ensuring that the spatial information and regulatory interactions within each chromosome are preserved. Within this framework, we established intra-chromosomal connections, employing separate neural network structures for each chromosome. At each hidden layer of these networks, we incorporated 64 and 32 units to capture the complexity and nuances of the chromatin landscape. Subsequently, we concatenated the output from all hidden layers and applied a final fully connected layer to reduce the dimensionality to 20, forming the latent representation. This elaborate design allows the model to capture intricate patterns and relationships within each chromosome, enhancing its ability to discern fine-grained details in the ATAC modality. The decoder mirrored this structure, utilizing a complementary design to faithfully reconstruct the input information from the condensed latent space.

###### Cross-modality mapping details

Starting from the 20-unit latent embedding obtained for each modality, we introduced an additional hidden layer with 128 units to further enrich the representation. This intermediate layer serves as a bottleneck for information integration, enabling the model to capture more complex relationships and dependencies within and between modalities. The inclusion of a hidden layer with a higher dimensionality facilitates the extraction of abstract features that contribute to a more comprehensive and expressive representation. Subsequently, we concatenated the output of this hidden layer with the 20-unit latent space of the target modality, forming a merged feature space that encapsulates both the enriched representation and the unique characteristics of the target modality. This strategic combination allows the model to fuse information from the source and target modalities, facilitating the translation process by leveraging shared patterns and capturing modality-specific nuances.

#### C.1.2 Training details

The training process of our model involves a staged approach, where we first focus on training the autoencoders ( F R Enc , F R Dec , F A Enc , F A Dec ) before transitioning to the translation units. This sequential training strategy allows us to leverage pre-trained autoencoders to initialize the translation modules, providing a solid foundation for subsequent learning. Specifically, we start by independently training the RNA and ATAC autoencoders, ensuring that each modality's unique features are effectively captured. Once the autoencoders have learned meaningful representations, we freeze their parameters to prevent further updates and shift our attention to training the translation functions f ( · ) and g ( · ) along with the discriminators D ( · ).

In the translation phase, the frozen autoencoder parameters act as fixed feature extractors, and the focus shifts to refining the transformation functions and discriminators. The use of a naive GAN in the adversarial loss, as opposed to the Wasserstein GAN (wGAN) loss, is a deliberate choice based on superior performance observed during validation. This choice aligns with the goal of achieving robust and effective translation while minimizing computational requirements. The iterative nature of this training approach strikes a balance between computational efficiency and model performance, allowing us to achieve competitive results without sacrificing training time or hardware resources.

#### C.1.3 Dataset processing details

In the preprocessing pipeline for all datasets, raw FASTQ files obtained from biological experiments underwent initial processing using CellRanger-arc (version 2.0.2) with the hg38 genome as the reference. Default parameters were applied to conduct the initial quality control. Subsequently, raw count matrices were generated, and further filtering was performed using the Pegasus package (version 1.7.1) for scRNA-seq data and the ArchR package (version 1.0.1) for scATAC-seq data. Cells with insufficient scRNA-seq reads ( &lt; 200), scATAC-seq sequencing depths ( &lt; 1000), or TSS enrichment ( &lt; 2) were excluded.

Addressing the issue of multiplets, where cells inadvertently combined during extraction, DoubletDetection (version 4.2) was employed to filter out likely multiplets in the scRNA-seq data. The resulting scRNA-seq data and scATAC-seq data were then processed to generate raw count matrices and chromatin accessibility matrices, respectively.

To prepare the datasets for training, further processing and baseline analysis were conducted on the two matrices. For scRNA-seq data, Pegasus normalization was performed using de- fault parameters, and the top 3,000 highly variable features (genes) were selected using the pegasus.highly variable features function. Principal Component Analysis (PCA) and Uniform Manifold Approximation and Projection (UMAP) were applied for visualization using the pegasus.pca() and pegasus.umap() functions. Cell type annotations were curated using an annotation pipeline with pegasus.infer cell types, pegasus.infer cluster names, and pegasus.annotate functions, leveraging tissue-specific marker genes associated with each dataset.

In the case of scATAC-seq data, due to the binary nature of chromatin accessibility (open or closed), the matrix was binarized. Visualization of initial QC results was performed using ArchR's addIterativeLSI and addUMAP functions. Feature selection was conducted using the TF-IDF method to address the dataset's high sparsity. Finally, LEIDEN clustering was performed using the addClusters function, and cell type annotations were manually assigned by overlaying marker gene enrichments on scATAC-seq clusters, capturing the most likely cell types for each cluster.
