### D.1 GRIDS Algorithm

Please refer to Algorithm 1 for the global feature importance explanation algorithm given the cross-modality surrogate.

###### Algorithm 1 GRIDS global feature importance explanation

Require:

cross-modality surrogate ˆ F

Require:

global feature explanation function L , gene to explain y j , values to perturb p

Ensure:

global feature explanation r ∗

- 1: initialize subset r at random

- 2: while not converged do

- 3: sample data ( x , y ) ∼ C

- 4: doing perturbation induced by r and p on the input data x

- 5: compute the global feature explanation following Eq. 2

- 6: estimate the indices transition T using Eq. 14

- 7: update the current r using the candidates in T using coordinate descent

- 8: end while

- 9: set optimal result r ∗ ← r

- 10: return r ∗

####### D.2 Datasets

#### D.2.1 Human Brain PFC Single-Cell Multimodal Dataset

We assembled a deeply sequenced single-cell multi-omic dataset from postmortem human pre-frontal cortex (PFC) collected by the PsychENCODE consortium [5]. After standard QC and initial processing with Cell Ranger ARC [298], the final dataset contains 10,266 cells profiled with paired ATAC-seq and RNA-seq. For ATAC-seq, we called 127,219 peaks using Macs2 [296] and constructed a 10 , 266 × 127 , 219 peak-by-cell matrix using ArchR [103], which we binarized to represent peak accessibility. For RNA-seq, reads were mapped to 19,607 genes/pseudogenes to form a 10 , 266 × 19 , 607 count matrix, which was normalized with Pegasus [154] and reduced to the top 3,000 differentially expressed genes, yielding a 10 , 266 × 3 , 000 expression matrix used for model training.

To support training and evaluation, we generated cell-type annotations independently for RNA-seq and ATAC-seq. RNA-seq labels were obtained by PCA (20 components) followed by LEIDEN clustering (resolution 1.0) [256], with clusters assigned to cell types using established marker genes [148]. ATAC-seq labels followed a similar pipeline, with an additional ArchR-based conversion to gene-activity matrices prior to clustering. Cells were annotated into eight major classes: Exc, SST, VIP, Astro, Endo, Micro, OPC, and Oligo, using marker panels such as ALDH1A1 / AQP4 / GJA1 (Astro), CLDN5 / FLT1 (Endo), APBB1IP / CX3CR1 (Micro), OLIG1 / OLIG2 (OPC), MOBP / MOG (Oligo), and GAD1 / GAD2 with SST or VIP for inhibitory subtypes.

#### D.2.2 MNIST Dataset

We followed the binary MNIST setup of Shrikumar et al. [229] to classify digits 8 versus 3, rescaling pixel intensities to [0 , 1] and splitting the dataset into train/validation/test subsets at 0.7/0.1/0.2. We trained a ReLU-based MLP (no batch normalization) using Adam [139] with learning rate 1 × 10 -4 and batch size 64 for up to 50 epochs, applying early stopping with a patience of 2000 steps based on validation loss, which achieved 97.9% test accuracy.

### D.3 Implementation Details

Our implementation is in PyTorch. For the cross-modality surrogate mapping, we use a fourlayer MLP with embedding dimension 32 and set the shared latent dimension to d h = 20. Adversarial alignment is weighted by γ = 0 . 3, and the number of discriminator pairs T matches the number of cell types in the dataset. During global explanation generation, we set β = 0 . 1 and use candidate size k = 32. We use the authors' reference implementations for LIME, CXPlain, and SAGE, while implementing Saliency, SmoothGrad, and FIMAP ourselves.

On the MNIST benchmark, LIME uses random sampling to generate 1024 neighbors per sample and cosine distance for neighbor similarity. For CXPlain, we explain non-overlapping 2 × 2 pixel regions, producing 14 × 14 attribution maps, and train the CXPlain (U-net) model for 500 epochs with batch size 512 using Adam (learning rate 5 × 10 -4 ). SAGE is trained for 300 epochs (batch size 512, Adam learning rate 10 -3 ) and uses 10,000 sampled permutations (batch size 512). For GRIDS on MNIST, we set subset size L = 64 and candidate size 32.

On the single-cell multimodal benchmark, LIME again uses 1024 randomly sampled neighbors with cosine distance. CXPlain is adapted to ATAC-seq by explaining non-overlapping sliding windows of size w = 4 peaks (also tested w ∈ 16 , 32 , 64), producing attribution maps of length 127 , 219 //w , and training a 1D-convolutional CXPlain (U-net) explainer for 2000 epochs (batch size 512, Adam learning rate 5 × 10 -4 ). For SAGE, we set the permutation budget to 256,000 samples (batch size 512) and train for 300 epochs (batch size 512, Adam learning rate 10 -3 ), though increasing sampling still did not lead to convergence. For GRIDS, we use subset size L = 10 or L = 128 depending on the experiment, with candidate size 32.

All experiments run on a server with an AMD EPYC 7662 64-core CPU, 1 TB RAM, 32 CPU cores allocated via Slurm, and eight NVIDIA RTX A6000 GPUs; each job typically requests 4 CPU cores, 1 GPU, and 90 GB memory. In terms of runtime, GRIDS generates global explanations with L = 64 on MNIST in under 3 minutes, and on the single-cell benchmark it requires about 3 minutes per marker-gene/cell-type pair for L = 10 and about 24 minutes for L = 128.

### D.4 Supplementary Results of Global Subset Perturbation in MNIST

To compare the global feature importance estimation performance of GRIDS with existing state-of-the-art methods, we evaluated its ability to identify globally important pixels on MNIST [152]. Following the binary digit classification setup of Shrikumar et al. [229], we trained models to distinguish two digit categories (8 vs 3). During preprocessing, pixel intensities were normalized to the range [0 , 1], and the dataset was randomly partitioned into training, validation, and test splits (see Appendix C and D for details). The resulting binary classifier achieved 97 . 9% accuracy on the held-out test set. We then applied a collection of global feature importance explanation methods to estimate which input pixels most strongly influence the classifier's predictions, learning explanations from the training set and representing each explanation as a subset by selecting the topL pixels according to the corresponding importance scores, with L = 64.

Figure D.1: Benchmarks of the distributions of the log odds changes on the test dataset after masking L = 64 most important pixels according to different explanations learned across the training dataset. Our method is better than CXPlain with p -value &lt; 0 . 004.

<!-- image -->

To ensure all methods are evaluated under the same perturbation protocol, we masked the selected pixels to zero (i.e., p = 0) and quantified the resulting change in model confidence. To assess generalization of the learned global importance patterns, we applied each method's subset to the test set and measured the shift in classification confidence using the change in log odds, defined as ∆ ϕ = E [ ϕ ( p ( x )) -ϕ ( p ( x \ r ))], where ϕ ( p ) = log( p ) -log(1 -p ) is the log-odds function, p ( x ) denotes the predicted class probability for the original image, and p ( x \ r ) is the probability after masking the globally important pixels. The distribution of log-odds changes is summarized in Figure D.1. In addition, we visualize the resulting global explanations and masked images in Figure D.2 to qualitatively assess whether each method identifies the key global features: when the estimation is precise, the masked image should resemble a '3' more than an '8', since pixels providing evidence for the digit being recognized as an 8 are expected to be removed.

Figure D.2: An examination of the most significant L = 64 pixels identified by various methods, is presented through their masked results on four distinct images in the test set. Our subset perturbation learning method can find a similar combinatorial pattern as SAGE.

<!-- image -->

### D.5 Supplementary Results on Cell-Type-Matched HiC Experiments

To independently validate our predicted CREs on the target gene GAD1 within the VIP cell type, we conducted an Hi-C experiment using the cell-type-matched data from PsychENCODE [79]. As shown in Figure D.3, the results show that the highlighted topological domains (TADs) on chromosome 2 containing GAD1 do contain the found linkages that connect the promotor and the enhancer of the target gene by GRIDS.

Figure D.3: GRIDS effectively finds cis-regulatory elements known to associate with cell-typespecific marker genes. (A) Hi-C topological domain (TAD) visualization of chromosome 2, with the specific TAD that contains GAD1 marked in red. (B) Visualization of: (top) inhibitory-specific chromatin accessibility; (center) the found enhancer-promoter linkages by GRIDS for GAD1; (bottom) the reference genome with GAD1 gene marked in red.
